<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

<a id="main:map"></a>
Let $m$ be normalized Lebesgue area on $\mathbb{T}^2=\mathbb{R}^2/\mathbb{Z}^2$. The standard sine map is the area-preserving diffeomorphism $$\label{main:map}
 f_k(x,y)=\bigl(x+y+k\sin(2\pi x),\;y+k\sin(2\pi x)\bigr)
 \pmod{\mathbb{Z}^2},\qquad k\in\mathbb{R}.$$ We consider its Kolmogorov–Sinai entropy $h_m(f_k)$ for this fixed invariant measure. The distinction between topological complexity and chaos visible to area is essential: a hyperbolic set may have zero area, and even full Hausdorff dimension does not imply positive area.

<a id="stmt-main-entropy"></a>
<div id="main:entropy" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There exists $k_0>0$ such that $$h_m(f_k)>0\qquad\text{for every }k\ge k_0.$$ Equivalently, for every such $k$ the largest Lyapunov exponent of $f_k$ is positive on a set of positive $m$-measure.*

</div>

In particular the parameter set with positive metric entropy contains an interval of positive length. Thus <a href="#main:entropy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:entropy">1</a> resolves affirmatively Sinai’s positive metric entropy conjecture in the formulation of Berger and Turaev <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[BergerTuraev2019](#ref-BergerTuraev2019), Conjecture 0.4], and gives the stronger all-sufficiently-large-parameter conclusion. This large-parameter formulation is also recorded by Obata <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Obata2021](#ref-Obata2021)]. Berger and Turaev’s recurrence $(x,y)\mapsto(2x-y+a\sin(2\pi x),x)$ is conjugate to <a href="#main:map" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:map">[main:map]</a> by $(x,y)\mapsto(x,x-y)$, with $a=k$. The assertion concerns both parameter measure and, at each parameter, phase-space area. It makes no claim of ergodicity, almost-everywhere positive exponents, or a uniform positive lower bound on entropy.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Context

The standard map arose as a model of conservative instability <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Chirikov1979](#ref-Chirikov1979)]. Its large-parameter regime combines strong local expansion with recurrent losses of derivative growth. Duarte’s large-parameter analysis constructs hyperbolic basic sets and exhibits elliptic-island and tangency phenomena in the standard family <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Duarte1994](#ref-Duarte1994)]. Gorodetski <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Gorodetski2012](#ref-Gorodetski2012)] obtained transitive invariant sets of full Hausdorff dimension for a residual set of large parameters. These results reveal extensive deterministic structure, but neither hyperbolic sets nor full dimension imply positive area; residual parameter sets also need not have positive Lebesgue measure.

Other entropy results use different measures or notions. Obata <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Obata2021](#ref-Obata2021)] established uniqueness of the measure of maximal entropy for sufficiently large standard-map parameters, without identifying that measure with area. Oliveira <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Oliveira2024](#ref-Oliveira2024), Theorem 5.1] proved positive topological entropy for every $k>0$ with $k\ne2/\pi$ in the normalization of <a href="#main:map" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:map">[main:map]</a>. Positive topological entropy does not by itself give positive entropy for the specific invariant measure $m$. Berger and Turaev <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[BergerTuraev2019](#ref-BergerTuraev2019)] proved Herman’s positive-entropy conjecture by conservative perturbations, including perturbations near standard maps; these are not restricted to amplitude changes in <a href="#main:map" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:map">[main:map]</a>. Related hyperbolicity is available for Anosov-driven skew products with standard-map fibers <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[BergerCarrasco2014](#ref-BergerCarrasco2014)] and randomly perturbed standard maps <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[BlumenthalXueYoung2017](#ref-BlumenthalXueYoung2017)]. Coupling, noise, and smooth perturbation change the dynamical system or its probability law. The question here concerns Lebesgue area for the exact, fixed sine map at each sufficiently large amplitude.

<a id="main:pesin"></a>
We use the established entropy formula, not a new entropy theory. For a smooth area-preserving diffeomorphism of the compact torus, the multiplicative ergodic theorem <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Oseledets1968](#ref-Oseledets1968)] gives almost-everywhere Lyapunov exponents. The determinant-one condition makes them $\lambda_+(z)$ and $-\lambda_+(z)$, with $\lambda_+(z)\ge0$. The entropy formula of Pesin, in the absolutely continuous invariant-measure form <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Pesin1977](#ref-Pesin1977), [Mane1981](#ref-Mane1981), [Mane1983Errata](#ref-Mane1983Errata)], gives $$\label{main:pesin}
 h_m(f_k)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^2}\lambda_+(z)\,\mathrm{d}m(z).$$ The map is $C^\infty$, the manifold is compact without boundary, and $m$ is an absolutely continuous invariant probability. The derivative and inverse-derivative logarithms are bounded at each fixed parameter, so all integrability hypotheses hold. Neither ergodicity nor a prior assumption of nonzero exponents is required. Consequently the task is to prove positivity of $\lambda_+$ on positive area.

<a id="stmt-main-components"></a>
<div id="main:components" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2** (Nonuniformly hyperbolic ergodic component). *For each $k\ge k_0$ there is a measurable $f_k$-invariant set $E=E(k)$ with $m(E)>0$ such that $(E,m(E)^{-1}m|_E,f_k)$ is ergodic and $f_k$ has one positive and one negative Lyapunov exponent almost everywhere on $E$. For some integer $N=N(k,E)\ge1$, there is a measurable partition $E=E_0\sqcup\cdots\sqcup E_{N-1}$ such that $f_k(E_j)=E_{j+1\bmod N}$ and $(E_j,m(E_j)^{-1}m|_{E_j},f_k^N)$ is Bernoulli for every $j$. All invariance and partition statements are modulo $m$-null sets; each $E_j$ has area $m(E)/N>0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-main-components"><summary>Proof of stmt-main-components</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $k\ge k_0$ and let $H_k$ be the invariant Oseledets set where $\lambda_+>0$. It has positive area by <a href="#main:entropy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:entropy">1</a>, while $\det Df_k=1$ makes the other exponent $-\lambda_+<0$ there. The normalized restriction $\nu_k=m(H_k)^{-1}m|_{H_k}$ is an absolutely continuous invariant probability on the compact torus, with no zero Lyapunov exponents. Pesin’s ergodic and Bernoulli decomposition theorems, as stated in <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[KatokBurns1994](#ref-KatokBurns1994), Theorems 3.3–3.4], apply to $\nu_k$ and give a positive-$\nu_k$ ergodic component $E$ and its finite cyclic partition. Since $\nu_k(E)=m(E)/m(H_k)>0$, the component has positive area; normalizing $\nu_k$ on $E$ or on a partition piece agrees with normalizing $m$. Area preservation gives $m(E_j)=m(E)/N$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Proof mechanism

The proof has two quantitative inputs and a multiscale conclusion. In transfer coordinates the derivative is a product of determinant-one matrices $T_{i,j}$. With the one-step bound $M=2\pi k+4$, put $g(i,j)=\log_M\left\lVert T_{i,j}\right\rVert$. The bound $0\le g(i,j)\le|i-j|$ and the triangle inequality make $g$ a stationary semimetric on orbit time. We track the expected shortfall $1-\mathbb{E}g(0,n)/n$ from maximal growth at each length $n$.

First, <a href="#can:theorem" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:theorem">4</a> bounds the probability that two long segments grow nearly at the maximal rate but their concatenation almost completely cancels. A cancellation supplies a linearized solution that is small at both endpoints. Matched expansion scales on its two sides give many local tests. A uniform analytic scarcity estimate, including the degenerate cases of nearly cancelling reciprocal cosines, converts those tests into exponential probability decay. Finite-time direction and tangency geometry in the standard family has precedents in <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[BloorLuzzatto2009](#ref-BloorLuzzatto2009)]; the matched-scale scarcity estimate used here is proved below, rather than imported from that work.

Second, <a href="#bridge:theorem" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:theorem">9</a> gives a weighted product estimate for observations near opposite ends of a rapidly growing segment. Finite Dirichlet problems construct two transverse contracting graph families. Their uniformly controlled slice Jacobians yield the estimate by changes of variables. This step does not presume mixing or independence of orbit segments. For comparison, Blumenthal <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Blumenthal2021](#ref-Blumenthal2021)] obtains fixed-parameter correlation bounds that are useful over a finite parameter-dependent time range. Those bounds do not supply the weighted estimate across fast bridges in <a href="#bridge:product" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:product">[bridge:product]</a>, which applies at arbitrary sufficiently large bridge lengths; its restriction to fast bridges is essential.

Suppose now that arbitrarily large parameters had zero exponent almost everywhere. The shortfall at every fixed length tends to zero as the parameter grows, whereas at each fixed zero-exponent parameter it tends to one as the length grows. Cancellation scarcity bounds how much this shortfall can jump when the length doubles. We can therefore select a range of dyadic lengths over which it first increases and normalize by the small terminal shortfall. Rescaling the distance arrays produces measures that are locally finite away from the affine arrays $d(s,t)=w|s-t|$, where every interval grows at one common linear rate. The bridge estimate restricts which slow intervals can coexist with a fast connecting segment. Together with cancellation scarcity and a four-point inequality, it leaves three kinds of nonaffine limit arrays: an entirely slow array, or one with one or two exterior unit-speed rays.

The final comparison uses a capped shortfall: it is linear with a small slope near zero, and equals one on uniformly slow intervals. Sum its whole-interval value minus the average of its two half-interval values over the selected dyadic lengths. This telescopes to a positive normalized terminal value, since the initial contribution vanishes. The limiting sum decomposes into the backward dilates of the terminal measure and a translation- and dilation-invariant remainder. The first contribution is strictly less than that same terminal value: a nonzero terminal measure retains a slow interval visible at arbitrarily fine scales. The invariant remainder’s contribution is nonpositive. For its two-ray shapes, translation and dilation averaging compare fine-scale occupancy of the slow interval with the smaller capped loss over long crossing intervals. These two bounds contradict the positive telescoping balance.

Passing to the limit in that balance is itself a proof obligation. Vague convergence away from affine arrays does not control weighted mass approaching them. The cap is flat at slow affine speeds; the bridge estimate removes intermediate affine speeds; and cancellation truncation controls the remaining unit-speed array. Thus the same quantitative inputs govern both the nonaffine shapes and the mass that might otherwise escape them.

fixes the transfer coordinates and growth conventions. [can:section](#can:section), [loc:section](#loc:section) prove cancellation scarcity and its local analytic estimate. [bridge:section](#bridge:section) supplies the finite-graph product estimate. [sec:scales](#sec:scales) constructs the dyadic limit measures; <a href="#shape:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:section">7</a> classifies their support, and <a href="#end:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="end:section">8</a> completes the capped-deficit balance. These quantitative ingredients are proved in the form needed here; no external standard-map entropy assertion or independence hypothesis is imported.

<a id="sec:setup"></a>
### Transfer matrices and growth shortfalls

Throughout the proof the parameter $k$ is positive and sufficiently large. Set <a id="setup:parameters"></a>$$\label{setup:parameters}
 K=2\pi k,\qquad M=K+4,\qquad
 \phi(x)=2x+k\sin(2\pi x),\qquad
 v(x)=\phi'(x)=2+K\cos(2\pi x).$$ Growth logarithms are to base $M$. Natural logarithms are retained in the definitions of entropy and Lyapunov exponents. Constants denoted by $C$ may increase from one occurrence to the next. Unless a dependence is specified, constants in large-$M$ estimates do not depend on the orbit, the integer times, or $M$.

<a id="setup:transfer"></a>
Write $(x_i,y_i)=f_k^i(z)$ for all $i\in\mathbb{Z}$, and put $q_i=x_i\in\mathbb{T}$. The identity $x_i-y_i=x_{i-1}$ shows that the fixed torus automorphism $(x,y)\mapsto(x,x-y)$ replaces the state by $(q_i,q_{i-1})$. It preserves normalized area, and the recurrence becomes <a id="setup:recurrence"></a>$$\label{setup:recurrence}
 q_{i+1}=\phi(q_i)-q_{i-1}\pmod1.$$ When varying an orbit locally we choose initial real lifts and iterate this same recurrence over $\mathbb{R}$, without reducing modulo one. Its linear recurrence is $$\label{setup:transfer}
 \binom{u_{i+1}}{u_i}
 =A_i\binom{u_i}{u_{i-1}},\qquad
 A_i=\begin{pmatrix}v(q_i)&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}.$$ For $a,b\in\mathbb{Z}$, let $T_{a,b}$ carry the linearized pair at time $a$ to the pair at time $b$. Thus $T_{a,c}=T_{b,c}T_{a,b}$ and $T_{b,a}=T_{a,b}^{-1}$.

<a id="setup:norm-bounds"></a>
All matrix and vector norms are Euclidean unless otherwise stated. Every transfer matrix has determinant one. A real two-dimensional determinant-one matrix has singular values $D,D^{-1}$ with $D\ge1$; in particular $$\label{setup:norm-bounds}
 \left\lVert T_{a,b}\right\rVert=\left\lVert T_{b,a}\right\rVert,\qquad
 1\le\left\lVert T_{a,b}\right\rVert\le M^{|b-a|}.$$ Indeed both $A_i$ and $A_i^{-1}$ have Euclidean norm at most $M$. The same bound holds in the maximum norm: their largest absolute row sum is at most $K+3<M$. Also <a id="setup:derivatives"></a>$$\label{setup:derivatives}
 |\phi'|\le M,\qquad |\phi^{(p)}|\le C_pM\quad(p\ge2)$$ for every fixed derivative order $p$. These estimates apply equally on all real lifts. The fixed change of coordinates does not alter Lyapunov exponents, since conjugating a derivative product by a fixed invertible matrix changes its logarithmic norm by a bounded amount.

The probability $\mathbb{P}$ and expectation $\mathbb{E}$ always refer to normalized area of the initial state. At every fixed time $i$, the pair $(q_i,q_{i-1})$ has uniform product distribution on $\mathbb{T}^2$. The process is stationary under all integer time shifts. This does not assert independence of coordinates observed at different times.

<a id="scale:distance-deficit"></a>
<a id="scale:distance-bound"></a>
We record growth and its expected shortfall by $$\label{scale:distance-deficit}
 g(i,j)=\log_M\left\lVert T_{i,j}\right\rVert,\qquad
 e_n=\mathbb{E}\left[1-\frac{g(0,n)}n\right]\quad(n\ge1).$$ The transfer identities make $g$ a stationary semimetric on orbit time: it is symmetric, vanishes on the diagonal, and satisfies the triangle inequality. Distinct times may have zero distance. The one-step bound gives $$\label{scale:distance-bound}
 0\le g(i,j)\le|i-j|,$$ so $e_n\in[0,1]$ measures the average loss from the upper growth rate over an interval of length $n$. These definitions do not assume that Lyapunov exponents vanish; that assumption enters only in the contradiction argument of <a href="#sec:scales" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:scales">6</a>.

<a id="setup:sine-triangle"></a>
For nonzero vectors $a,b\in\mathbb{R}^2$, the symbol $a\wedge b$ denotes their determinant. For unit vectors its absolute value is the absolute sine of their angle and depends only on their unoriented lines. The absolute sines satisfy $$\label{setup:sine-triangle}
 |a\wedge c|\le |a\wedge b|+|b\wedge c|$$ for unit vectors: express the two outer vectors in the orthonormal basis given by $b$ and a perpendicular vector, and expand their determinant.

The following elementary observation will repeatedly convert a small total growth deficit into good growth on many subintervals.

<a id="stmt-setup-shortfall-count"></a>
<div id="setup:shortfall-count" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Shortfall count). *Let $z_0,\ldots,z_N$ be real numbers with $z_j-z_{j-1}\le1$, and set $$\mathcal D=N-(z_N-z_0)
 =\sum_{j=1}^N\bigl(1-z_j+z_{j-1}\bigr).$$ For $c<1$, the number of indices $j$ for which some $0\le l<j$ satisfies $z_j-z_l<c(j-l)$ is at most $C\mathcal D/(1-c)$, with an absolute constant $C$. The same conclusion holds for the number of indices $j$ with some $j<l\le N$ satisfying $z_l-z_j<c(l-j)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-setup-shortfall-count"><summary>Proof of stmt-setup-shortfall-count</summary>

*Proof.* For each bad index choose one witnessing interval. Its length is a positive integer, and the sum of the nonnegative step shortfalls over it exceeds $(1-c)$ times its length. Select disjoint intervals greedily in decreasing order of length, discarding each interval that intersects one already selected. Regard intervals as half-open when deciding disjointness, so that the sums of shortfalls over selected intervals have disjoint sets of steps.

Every discarded interval meets a selected interval of at least its length and is contained in the concentric threefold enlargement of that selected interval. The bad endpoints are therefore covered by these enlargements. An interval of integer length $L\ge1$ has at most $3L+1$ integer points in its threefold enlargement. If the selected lengths are $L_1,\ldots,L_s$, the number of bad indices is at most $4\sum_r L_r$. Disjointness and nonnegativity give $(1-c)\sum_rL_r\le\mathcal D$. This proves the first statement, and the same selection applied to the forward witnessing intervals proves the second. ◻

</details>

We will use the count both for logarithms of vector norms, whose one-step increments are at most one by <a href="#setup:norm-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="setup:norm-bounds">[setup:norm-bounds]</a>, and for the logarithms of successive maximum norms of solutions of <a href="#setup:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="setup:transfer">[setup:transfer]</a>. Neither use requires a lower bound on each individual increment.

<a id="can:section"></a>
### Nearly full-rate cancellations

The first estimate concerns two long products which expand rapidly when viewed from their common endpoint, although their concatenation has little growth. Such a configuration forces a linearized solution to be small at both distant endpoints. We use this solution to locate many pairs of comparable expansion scales on the two sides of the common endpoint.

<a id="stmt-can-theorem"></a>
<div id="can:theorem" class="theorem">

<a id="can:event-bound"></a>
**Theorem 4** (Cancellation estimate). *There are constants $\delta>0$, $C<\infty$, $\gamma>0$, and $M_0<\infty$ such that, for every $M\ge M_0$, every $i\in\mathbb{Z}$, and every $n\in\mathbb{N}$, $$\label{can:event-bound}
\mathbb{P}\left\{
\begin{array}{l}
\min\bigl(\log_M\left\lVert T_{i,i-n}\right\rVert,
          \log_M\left\lVert T_{i,i+n}\right\rVert\bigr)\ge(1-\delta)n,\\[2pt]
\log_M\left\lVert T_{i-n,i+n}\right\rVert\le\delta n
\end{array}
\right\}
\le C\exp(-\gamma n).$$ All four constants are independent of the center, the length, and the parameter $M$ in the indicated range.*

</div>

Throughout this section and the next, fix <a id="can:c"></a>$$\label{can:c}
c=\frac{99}{100}.$$ Constants used before the final choice of $\delta$ are independent of $\delta$, $n$, and $M$, unless a dependence is stated explicitly.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### A solution small at both endpoints

<a id="can:small-solution"></a>
By stationarity it is enough to consider $i=0$. Suppose the event in <a href="#can:event-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:event-bound">[can:event-bound]</a> occurs. Write $$D_- = \left\lVert T_{0,-n}\right\rVert,\qquad D_+ = \left\lVert T_{0,n}\right\rVert,$$ and let $a_-$ and $a_+$ be unit most-expanded input directions for these two products. If $b$ is a unit vector perpendicular to $a_-$, then $$\left\lVert T_{0,-n}b\right\rVert=D_-^{-1},\qquad
\left\lVert T_{0,n}b\right\rVert\ge D_+\left\lvert a_-\wedge a_+\right\rvert.$$ The first equality uses determinant one: the singular values of either product are its norm and the reciprocal of its norm. Applying the full product to the vector $T_{0,-n}b$ gives $$\left\lVert T_{-n,n}\right\rVert
\ge D_-D_+\left\lvert a_-\wedge a_+\right\rvert,
\qquad
\left\lvert a_-\wedge a_+\right\rvert\le M^{-(2-3\delta)n}.$$ Resolving $b$ in the singular input directions of $T_{0,n}$ also gives the upper bound $$\left\lVert T_{0,n}b\right\rVert
\le D_+^{-1}+D_+\left\lvert a_-\wedge a_+\right\rvert
\le 2M^{-(1-3\delta)n}.$$ The left endpoint has the smaller bound $D_-^{-1}$. Thus the solution of the linearized recurrence with central pair $b$ is small at both endpoints. One of its entries at times $0$ and $-1$ has absolute value at least $1/\sqrt2$. Choose that time $e\in\{0,-1\}$ and divide the whole solution by its value there. The normalized solution satisfies $$\label{can:small-solution}
u_e=1,\qquad \left\lVert(u_0,u_{-1})\right\rVert\le\sqrt2,\qquad
\left\lVert(u_n,u_{n-1})\right\rVert,\ \left\lVert(u_{-n},u_{-n-1})\right\rVert
\le 3M^{-(1-3\delta)n}.$$ This construction will be used only as an existence statement; no measurable selection of a singular direction is required.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Outward paths and good indices

<a id="can:wronskian"></a>
<a id="can:center-equation"></a>
Fix $e$ and $q_e$. Use $\theta=q_{e-1}$ as the other circle coordinate, so that $q_{e+1}=\phi(q_e)-\theta$ modulo one. The two outward paths are $$q_j^-=q_{e-j},\qquad q_j^+=q_{e+j}\qquad(j\ge0).$$ On either path the positions and every linearized solution obey the same second-order recurrence. In calculations involving just one path, omit its sign and write $q_j,u_j,v_j=v(q_j)$, with $u_0=1$. Define <a id="can:t-definition"></a>$$\label{can:t-definition}
t_0=0,\qquad t_1=1,\qquad t_{j+1}=v_jt_j-t_{j-1}.$$ For local real lifts of the positions, $$\partial_\theta q_j^-=t_j^-,\qquad
\partial_\theta q_j^+=-t_j^+.$$ The sign difference comes solely from the initial positions at index one. It does not change the recurrence or its Wronskian identity: $$\label{can:wronskian}
u_{j-1}t_j-u_jt_{j-1}=1\qquad(j\ge1).$$ Indeed the expression on the left is one at $j=1$ and the recurrence preserves it. A common normalized solution on the two paths has $$\label{can:center-equation}
u_1^-+u_1^+=v(q_e).$$ Conversely, two outward solutions with value one at index zero and satisfying <a href="#can:center-equation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:center-equation">[can:center-equation]</a> assemble into a solution of the original recurrence across the center.

<a id="can:log-increments"></a>
On each path the two small endpoint entries in <a href="#can:small-solution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:small-solution">[can:small-solution]</a> are indexed by $N-1,N$. More explicitly, the forward and backward lengths are $N_+=n-e$ and $N_-=n+e+1$, respectively: one is $n$ and the other is $n+1$. Define pair-growth logarithms $$\tau_j=\log_M\max(\left\lvert t_j\right\rvert,\left\lvert t_{j-1}\right\rvert),\qquad
\upsilon_j=\log_M\max(\left\lvert u_j\right\rvert,\left\lvert u_{j-1}\right\rvert)
\quad(1\le j\le N).$$ Both pairs are nonzero, so these logarithms are defined. The bounds for one step and its inverse in the maximum norm give $$\label{can:log-increments}
\left\lvert\tau_{j+1}-\tau_j\right\rvert\le1,\qquad
\left\lvert\upsilon_{j+1}-\upsilon_j\right\rvert\le1.$$

<a id="stmt-can-good-definition"></a>
<div id="can:good-definition" class="definition">

<a id="can:good"></a>
**Definition 5**. An index $2\le j\le N-2$ is *good* for the selected normalized solution if $$\label{can:good}
\begin{aligned}
\tau_j-\tau_l&\ge c(j-l)&& (1\le l<j),\\
\tau_{j+1}-\tau_j&\ge c,\\
\upsilon_j-\upsilon_{j+1}&\ge c,
&\upsilon_{j+1}-\upsilon_{j+2}&\ge c.
\end{aligned}$$ For a path extending beyond a selected index, the same inequalities define goodness without reference to a particular terminal length $N$.

</div>

<a id="stmt-can-many-good"></a>
<div id="can:many-good" class="lemma">

<a id="can:bad-count"></a>
**Lemma 6**. *There is a constant $A$ such that, on each outward path associated to <a href="#can:small-solution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:small-solution">[can:small-solution]</a>, the number of indices in $\{2,\ldots,N-2\}$ which are not good is at most $$\label{can:bad-count}
\frac{A(\delta n+1)}{1-c}.$$ The estimate holds uniformly for $M\ge e$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-can-many-good"><summary>Proof of stmt-can-many-good</summary>

*Proof.* At index $N$, <a href="#can:wronskian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:wronskian">[can:wronskian]</a> and the endpoint bound imply $$1\le 2\max(\left\lvert u_N\right\rvert,\left\lvert u_{N-1}\right\rvert)
          \max(\left\lvert t_N\right\rvert,\left\lvert t_{N-1}\right\rvert),$$ and hence $$\tau_N\ge(1-3\delta)n-\log_M6,
\qquad
\upsilon_N\le-(1-3\delta)n+\log_M3.$$ Since $\tau_1=0$, $\upsilon_1\ge0$, and $N-1\le n$, the total shortfalls of the paths $\tau$ and $-\upsilon$ satisfy $$\begin{aligned}
\sum_{j=1}^{N-1}\bigl[1-(\tau_{j+1}-\tau_j)\bigr]
&\le3\delta n+\log6,\\
\sum_{j=1}^{N-1}\bigl[1-(\upsilon_j-\upsilon_{j+1})\bigr]
&\le3\delta n+\log3.
\end{aligned}$$ Every summand is nonnegative by <a href="#can:log-increments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:log-increments">[can:log-increments]</a>. Apply <a href="#setup:shortfall-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="setup:shortfall-count">3</a> to $\tau$ to count indices where the first line of <a href="#can:good" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:good">[can:good]</a> fails. An individual increment smaller than $c$ costs at least $1-c$ from the relevant shortfall sum. This counts failures of the second line; applied to $-\upsilon$, it counts failures of either of the two inequalities in the last line, with a factor of at most two because a single increment can be tested at two neighboring indices. Summing these bounds proves <a href="#can:bad-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:bad-count">[can:bad-count]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="can:good-t"></a>
At a good index let $L_j=\left\lvert t_j\right\rvert$. The first line of <a href="#can:good" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:good">[can:good]</a> with $l=j-1$ makes $t_j$ the dominant entry of its pair, and the next increase makes $t_{j+1}$ dominant at the next pair. Consequently $$\label{can:good-t}
\begin{gathered}
L_j=M^{\tau_j},\qquad
\left\lvert t_l\right\rvert\le L_jM^{-c(j-l)}\quad(1\le l<j),\\
\left\lvert t_{j+1}\right\rvert\ge M^cL_j.
\end{gathered}$$ The two decreases for the $u$-pairs similarly give $$\left\lvert u_{j+1}\right\rvert\le M^{-c}\left\lvert u_j\right\rvert
\le M^{-2c}\left\lvert u_{j-1}\right\rvert.$$ Using <a href="#can:wronskian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:wronskian">[can:wronskian]</a> and $\left\lvert t_{j-1}/t_j\right\rvert\le M^{-c}$, we obtain, after increasing a fixed lower bound for $M$, <a id="can:good-u"></a>$$\label{can:good-u}
\left\lvert u_{j-1}\right\rvert L_j
\le 1+M^{-2c}\left\lvert u_{j-1}\right\rvert L_j,
\qquad \left\lvert u_{j-1}\right\rvert\le\frac2{L_j}.$$

<a id="can:boundary-expansion"></a>
For $t_j\ne0$, let $R_j$ be the initial value at index one of the solution having value one at index zero and value zero at index $j$. This solution exists and is unique: changing its initial value at index one by $a$ changes its value at index $j$ by $at_j$. Its value at index $j-1$ is $1/t_j$, by <a href="#can:wronskian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:wronskian">[can:wronskian]</a>. Its value at index $j+1$ is therefore $-1/t_j$, which proves $$R_{j+1}-R_j=\frac1{t_jt_{j+1}}.$$ For any normalized solution, varying its initial value at index one also gives $u_1-R_{j+1}=u_{j+1}/t_{j+1}$. At a good index, [can:good-t,can:good-u] [can:good-t](#can:good-t), [can:good-u](#can:good-u) thus yield $$\label{can:boundary-expansion}
\begin{aligned}
u_1
&=R_{j+1}+\frac{u_{j+1}}{t_{j+1}}\\
&=R_j+\frac1{t_j^2(v_j-t_{j-1}/t_j)}
  +O\left(\frac{M^{-3c}}{L_j^2}\right).
\end{aligned}$$ The implied constant is absolute and does not depend on the index.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Matching the two expansion scales

depicts the two outward paths and one selected pair with comparable logarithmic derivative scales, as in the following lemma.

<a id="stmt-can-matching"></a>
<div id="can:matching" class="lemma">

<a id="can:matching-condition"></a>
**Lemma 7**. *There are fixed constants $\delta_0>0$, $c_1>0$, $C_1<\infty$, and $n_*<\infty$ with the following property. If $0<\delta\le\delta_0$ is fixed, increasing $n_*$ if necessary, every configuration in <a href="#can:small-solution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:small-solution">[can:small-solution]</a> with $n\ge n_*$ has at least $c_1n$ pairs $(j_m^+,j_m^-)$ of good indices such that both index sequences increase strictly and $$\label{can:matching-condition}
\left\lvert\tau_{j_m^+}^+-\tau_{j_m^-}^-\right\rvert\le\frac35.$$ The collection of all possible increasing index-pair patterns for a given $n$ has cardinality at most $\exp(C_1n)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-can-matching"><summary>Proof of stmt-can-matching</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a small fixed $\beta>0$, for example $\beta=10^{-3}$. By <a href="#can:bad-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:bad-count">[can:bad-count]</a>, choosing $\delta_0$ sufficiently small and then $n_*$ sufficiently large makes the bad-index count on each path at most $\beta n$. The total-shortfall estimate also gives, at every index, $$j-1-3\delta n-\log6\le\tau_j\le j-1.$$ Shrink $\delta_0$ once more and increase $n_*$ so that, for $n/3\le j\le n/2$, the corresponding $\tau_j$ lies in $[n/4,3n/4]$, while on the minus path $$\tau_{N-2}^-\ge\tau_N^--2>3n/4+1.$$ There are at least $n/6-2-\beta n$ good plus indices in this range.

For each such index put $a=\tau_j^+$. Starting from $\tau_1^-=0$, let $k$ be the first minus index with $\tau_k^-\ge a$. We have $\tau_{k-1}^-<a\le\tau_k^-$ and $\tau_k^--\tau_{k-1}^-\le1$. Thus at least one of these two values is within $1/2$ of $a$. The chosen index is in $\{2,\ldots,N-2\}$: the large lower bound on $a$ excludes the first two indices, and the displayed lower bound at $N-2$ provides the upper bound. If the chosen minus index is bad, discard this plus index.

Good plus indices $j<j'$ have $\tau_{j'}^+-\tau_j^+\ge c(j'-j)$. At most two such logarithms can belong to an interval of length one, since $2c>1$. A fixed bad minus index can therefore be responsible for at most two discarded plus indices. At least $n/6-2-3\beta n$ candidates remain.

Thin these candidates in increasing plus order by retaining the first one, then retaining the next whose plus logarithm is at least two larger than the last retained logarithm, and continuing. Since successive candidate logarithms are separated by at least $c$, each retention removes at most three candidates. A fixed positive fraction of $n$ remains; for example, decreasing the fraction if necessary, one may fix $c_1=10^{-2}$. The matched minus logarithms increase strictly, because each is within $1/2$ of the corresponding plus logarithm and the retained plus logarithms have gaps at least two. For two good minus indices, increasing index implies strictly increasing logarithm by <a href="#can:good" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:good">[can:good]</a>. Strict increase of their logarithms therefore forces strict increase of their indices as well. The matching error is at most $1/2$, which implies <a href="#can:matching-condition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:matching-condition">[can:matching-condition]</a>.

Each path contains at most $n+1$ available indices. An increasing paired pattern is determined by a subset of the indices on each path, with the two subsets of equal cardinality paired in order. There are at most $2^{2n+2}$ such choices. Enlarging an absolute $C_1$ gives the stated exponential bound. ◻

</details>

<figure id="can:wing-figure">

<figcaption>The two paths are followed outward from a fixed central position. Matched indices need not be equally far from the center; their derivatives with respect to the other central coordinate have comparable logarithmic size.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-can-wing-figure"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`can:wing-figure`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

% definitions used by this figure, copied from the source preamble

\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,>=Stealth]
  \draw[->] (0,0)--(-5,0);
  \draw[->] (0,0)--(5,0);
  \foreach \x in {-4.6,-2,0,2.6,4.6}
    \fill (\x,0) circle (1.5pt);
  \node[above] at (0,0) {$q_e$};
  \node[above] at (-2,0) {$q_{e-j_m^-}$};
  \node[above] at (2.6,0) {$q_{e+j_m^+}$};
  \node[above] at (-4.6,0) {left endpoint};
  \node[above] at (4.6,0) {right endpoint};
  \draw[densely dashed] (-2,-.15)--(-2,-.65)--(2.6,-.65)--(2.6,-.15);
  \node[below,align=center] at (0,-.65)
    {a matched pair: $\abs{\log_M L_{j_m^-}^- -\log_M L_{j_m^+}^+}\le3/5$};
