Visual witness description of PDF page 10 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A horizontal axis/line depicting outward paths from a central position labeled $q_e$, with left- and right-pointing arrows along the line. Marked points from left to right: "left endpoint", $q_{e-j_m^-}$, $q_e$, $q_{e+j_m^+}$, and "right endpoint". Below the points $q_{e-j_m^-}$ and $q_{e+j_m^+}$, an indicated pair is labeled "a matched pair: $\left|\log_M L_{j_m^-}^- - \log_M L_{j_m^+}^+\right| \le 3/5$".
```


<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### The local input

The analytic fact used at each matched pair is the following uniform scarcity statement. Its proof occupies <a href="#loc:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:section">4</a>.

<a id="stmt-can-local-scarcity"></a>
<div id="can:local-scarcity" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (Local scarcity). *For every $\eta>0$ there are $\eta'>0$ and $M_\eta<\infty$ such that the following holds for $M\ge M_\eta$. Fix $q_e$, a base coordinate $\theta_*$, and indices $j_+,j_-\ge2$ on the two outward paths. Suppose both indices are good at $\theta_*$ for a common normalized solution, and their logarithms satisfy <a href="#can:matching-condition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:matching-condition">[can:matching-condition]</a>. Let $$L_+=\left\lvert t_{j_+}^+(\theta_*)\right\rvert,\qquad
L_-=\left\lvert t_{j_-}^-(\theta_*)\right\rvert,\qquad L=\max(L_+,L_-).$$ Let $\mathcal S\subset[-1,1]$ consist of the $h$ for which, at $\theta=\theta_*+h/L$, the same two indices are good for some common solution with $u_e=1$. The solution is allowed to depend on $h$, and neither endpoint smallness nor a new matching condition is required at these other points. Then <a id="can:scarcity-bound"></a>$$\label{can:scarcity-bound}
\left\lvert\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,\mathcal S)<\eta'\}\right\rvert\le\eta.$$ Here and below the size of a subset of the line or circle denotes its Lebesgue measure. The constants are uniform over the central position, the base coordinate, and the selected indices.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### From local scarcity to exponential decay

<details id="proof-stmt-can-local-scarcity"><summary>Proof of stmt-can-local-scarcity</summary>

<a id="can:eta-choice"></a>
<a id="can:radius-choice"></a>
*Proof of <a href="#can:theorem" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:theorem">4</a>.* Fix once and for all a sufficiently small $\delta>0$ allowed by <a href="#can:matching" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:matching">7</a>. Its constants $c_1,C_1,n_*$ are now fixed. Choose $\eta>0$ so small that $$\label{can:eta-choice}
5\eta<1,\qquad
c_1\log\frac1{5\eta}>C_1+1.$$ Obtain $\eta'$ and $M_\eta$ from <a href="#can:local-scarcity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:local-scarcity">8</a>. Increase a fixed lower bound $M_0$ so that all estimates above hold, that $M_0\ge M_\eta$, and that $$\label{can:radius-choice}
M_0^{-c}<\frac14,\qquad M_0^{-c}/5<\eta'.$$ These choices are independent of $n$, of the center, and of $M\ge M_0$.

Fix $e\in\{0,-1\}$ and $q_e$. It is enough to consider patterns containing exactly $r=\lceil c_1n\rceil$ pairs, since a longer pattern can be truncated. For a fixed such increasing pattern, let $\mathcal F$ be the set of $\theta$ admitting one common normalized solution with all of the following properties: the original central pair has norm at most $\sqrt2$; both endpoint pairs satisfy <a href="#can:small-solution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:small-solution">[can:small-solution]</a>; and every pair of selected indices is good and satisfies <a href="#can:matching-condition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:matching-condition">[can:matching-condition]</a>. We emphasize that one solution must satisfy all conditions of a pattern at once.

The set $\mathcal F$ is measurable, in fact compact. To see this without selecting solutions, parameterize the original central pair by its entry other than the fixed entry $u_e=1$. The norm bound puts that parameter in $[-1,1]$. All later solution entries and all recurrence coefficients depend continuously on this parameter and on the circle coordinate $\theta$. Each pair entering a logarithm is nonzero, by invertibility of the recurrence. The endpoint, goodness, and matching conditions are closed inequalities on the compact product of the circle with $[-1,1]$. Their projection is therefore compact.

For $\theta\in\mathcal F$ and $1\le m\le r$, define $$L_m(\theta)=\max\left(
\left\lvert t_{j_m^+}^+(\theta)\right\rvert,\left\lvert t_{j_m^-}^-(\theta)\right\rvert\right),
\qquad s_m(\theta)=L_m(\theta)^{-1},$$ and let $$V_m=\bigcup_{\theta\in\mathcal F}
 B\bigl(\theta,s_m(\theta)/10\bigr)$$ be an open subset of the circle. Goodness at the later of two selected indices gives, separately on both paths, $$\left\lvert t_{j_{m+1}^\pm}^\pm(\theta)\right\rvert
\ge M^{c(j_{m+1}^\pm-j_m^\pm)}
      \left\lvert t_{j_m^\pm}^\pm(\theta)\right\rvert.$$ Since both index sequences increase strictly, <a id="can:radius-decay"></a>$$\label{can:radius-decay}
s_{m+1}(\theta)\le M^{-c}s_m(\theta),
\qquad s_m(\theta)\le M^{-c}.$$ For fixed $M,n$, these positive radii also have a positive lower bound: the one-step norm bound gives $\left\lvert t_j\right\rvert\le M^{j-1}\le M^n$ at all indices in question, so $s_m\ge M^{-n}$.

We claim <a id="can:cover-contraction"></a>$$\label{can:cover-contraction}
\left\lvert V_{m+1}\right\rvert\le5\eta\left\lvert V_m\right\rvert\qquad(1\le m<r).$$ Select disjoint balls from the family defining $V_m$ as follows. At each stage select a remaining ball whose radius is at least half the supremum of the remaining radii, and remove all remaining balls meeting it. Assign each removed ball to this selected ball. All selected balls are disjoint and have a fixed positive lower radius, so only finitely many can be selected on the circle; the procedure must exhaust the family. Every assigned ball meets its selected ball and has radius at most twice that of the selected ball.

Consider one selected center $\theta_*$ and write $s=s_m(\theta_*)$. The centers of its assigned balls are at circle distance at most $3s/10$ from $\theta_*$. The window of radius $s$ is an injective lift interval by [can:radius-choice,can:radius-decay] [can:radius-choice](#can:radius-choice), [can:radius-decay](#can:radius-decay). All assigned centers belong to $\mathcal F$, so they are successful for the same selected pair of indices in the local test based at $\theta_*$. A next-level ball centered at an assigned $\theta$ has radius at most $$\frac{s_{m+1}(\theta)}{10}
\le \frac{M^{-c}s_m(\theta)}{10}
\le \frac{M^{-c}s}{5}.$$ After rescaling the lifted coordinate by $1/s$, the union of all these next-level balls is contained in the $\eta'$-neighborhood of the successful set in <a href="#can:local-scarcity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:local-scarcity">8</a>, by <a href="#can:radius-choice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:radius-choice">[can:radius-choice]</a>. It therefore has unscaled length at most $\eta s$. Summing over selected balls proves $$\left\lvert V_{m+1}\right\rvert\le\eta\sum_{\text{selected}}s
=5\eta\sum_{\text{selected}}
  \left\lvert B(\theta_*,s/10)\right\rvert
\le5\eta\left\lvert V_m\right\rvert,$$ as claimed. The ball selected at a center is included in its own assigned group, so no next-level balls have been omitted.

Since $\mathcal F\subset V_m$ for every $m$ and $\left\lvert V_1\right\rvert\le1$, iteration gives $$\left\lvert\mathcal F\right\rvert\le(5\eta)^{r-1}
\le(5\eta)^{-1}
  \exp\left(-c_1n\log\frac1{5\eta}\right).$$ There are at most $\exp(C_1n)$ patterns. By <a href="#can:eta-choice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:eta-choice">[can:eta-choice]</a>, their union has circle measure at most $C\exp(-\gamma n)$ for a fixed $\gamma>0$, uniformly in the fixed coordinate $q_e$. The coordinate pair $(q_e,q_{e-1})$ has normalized product Lebesgue law. Integrating the conditional bound over $q_e$ and summing the two choices of $e$ therefore gives the same form of bound for the original event, for every $n\ge n_*$. Every point of that event is covered, by [can:small-solution,can:matching] [can:small-solution](#can:small-solution), [can:matching](#can:matching). Finally enlarge $C$ to cover the finitely many positive integers $n<n_*$, whose event probabilities are at most one. Stationarity restores every center $i$. ◻

</details>

<a id="loc:section"></a>
### Local scarcity at matched scales

This section proves <a href="#can:local-scarcity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:local-scarcity">8</a>. We use its two outward paths and the notation established in the cancellation argument. In particular, $c=99/100$, the central coordinate is fixed, and the variable is $\theta=q_{e-1}$. The selected indices are denoted by $j_+$ and $j_-$. Every assertion concerning an interval in $\theta$ uses real lifts of the recurrence. The constants below are independent of the selected indices and of the base configuration.

The central relation in <a href="#can:center-equation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:center-equation">[can:center-equation]</a> will become, after rescaling, an approximate identity between a cubic polynomial and two shifted reciprocal-cosine terms. A positive-measure Hausdorff limit of the closures of successful sets would force an analytic identity. We rule out that identity even when the two reciprocal terms nearly cancel or one phase slope tends to zero. The derivative estimates in the first two subsections provide the uniform remainders needed for these two degeneracies.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Control on a single rescaled path

<a id="loc:base-growth"></a>
<a id="loc:first-derivatives"></a>
<a id="loc:higher-derivatives"></a>
Fix either outward path and write $j$ for its selected index. Put $$L_j=\left\lvert t_j(\theta_*)\right\rvert,\qquad
 \xi=L_j(\theta-\theta_*),\qquad D=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}\xi}.$$ The initial position $q_0$ is constant and $q_1$ is affine in $\xi$, with derivative $\pm L_j^{-1}$. By <a href="#can:good-t" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:good-t">[can:good-t]</a>, the base linearized solution satisfies $$\frac{\left\lvert t_l(\theta_*)\right\rvert}{L_j}
 \le M^{-c(j-l)}\quad(1\le l\le j).
 \label{loc:base-growth}$$ We will prove, throughout $\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert\le1$, that $$\begin{aligned}
 \left\lvert q_l(\xi)-q_l(0)\right\rvert+\left\lvert Dq_l(\xi)\right\rvert
 &\le C M^{-c(j-l)}, \label{loc:first-derivatives}\\
 \left\lvert D^p q_l(\xi)\right\rvert
 &\le C_p M^{-pc(j-l)}M^{1-pc}
 \quad(2\le p\le5), \label{loc:higher-derivatives}
\end{aligned}$$ for $0\le l\le j$. The terms with $l=0$ vanish.

<a id="loc:nonlinear-comparison"></a>
Write $B_l=M^{-c(j-l)}$ and $\Delta_l=q_l(\xi)-q_l(0)$. Taylor’s formula for the recurrence, followed by propagation with the base linearized matrices, gives $$\left\lvert\Delta_l\right\rvert
 \le \left\lvert\xi\right\rvert\frac{\left\lvert t_l(\theta_*)\right\rvert}{L_j}
       +C\sum_{d=1}^{l-1}M^{l-d}\left\lvert\Delta_d\right\rvert^2.
 \label{loc:nonlinear-comparison}$$ Here the quadratic error at site $d$ is at most $CM\left\lvert\Delta_d\right\rvert^2$, and the remaining $l-d-1$ steps have norm at most $M^{l-d-1}$. For $l=1$ the estimate $\left\lvert\Delta_l\right\rvert\le2B_l$ follows directly from <a href="#loc:base-growth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:base-growth">[loc:base-growth]</a>. If it holds for all earlier indices, the sum in <a href="#loc:nonlinear-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:nonlinear-comparison">[loc:nonlinear-comparison]</a> is bounded by $$4C B_l^2\sum_{a\ge1}M^{(1-2c)a}.$$ Since $B_l\le1$ and $1-2c<0$, this is at most $B_l$ for all sufficiently large $M$. Induction proves the required displacement bound, uniformly in $j$.

Compare $Dq_l(\xi)$ with the base variation $\pm t_l(\theta_*)/L_j$. Their initial data agree. Their difference is propagated by the base matrices with forcing $$\bigl(v(q_d(\xi))-v(q_d(0))\bigr)Dq_d(\xi),$$ whose absolute value is at most $CM\left\lvert\Delta_d\right\rvert\left\lvert Dq_d\right\rvert$. Induction with $\left\lvert Dq_d\right\rvert\le2B_d$ therefore uses the same geometric sum and proves <a href="#loc:first-derivatives" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:first-derivatives">[loc:first-derivatives]</a>.

For completeness, the higher derivative induction uses the recurrence at the varied orbit. For $p\ge2$ it has the form $$D^p q_{l+1}=v(q_l)D^p q_l-D^p q_{l-1}+F_{p,l},$$ where $F_{p,l}$ is a finite sum, with coefficients depending only on $p$, of terms $$\phi^{(s)}(q_l)\prod_{i=1}^{s}D^{m_i}q_l,
 \qquad 2\le s\le p,\quad m_i\ge1,\quad
 \sum_{i=1}^{s}m_i=p.$$ Each $m_i$ is strictly smaller than $p$. The first derivative bound and the induction hypothesis give $\left\lvert D^{m_i}q_l\right\rvert\le C_{m_i}B_l^{m_i}$, since $M^{1-m_ic}\le1$ whenever $m_i\ge2$. Consequently $\left\lvert F_{p,l}\right\rvert\le C_p M B_l^p$. The initial higher derivatives at sites $0,1$ vanish. Propagation by the varied matrices gives $$\left\lvert D^p q_l\right\rvert
 \le C_p\sum_{d=1}^{l-1}M^{l-d}B_d^p
 \le C_p B_l^p\sum_{a\ge1}M^{(1-pc)a}
 \le C'_p B_l^p M^{1-pc}.$$ This proves <a href="#loc:higher-derivatives" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:higher-derivatives">[loc:higher-derivatives]</a> successively for $p=2,3,4,5$.

<a id="loc:varied-t"></a>
<a id="loc:quotient"></a>
At the selected site $Dq_j(0)=\pm1$. The case $p=2$ shows that $$Dq_j(\xi)=Dq_j(0)+O(M^{1-2c}).$$ It follows, for all sufficiently large $M$, that $$\left\lvert t_j(\xi)\right\rvert\ge L_j/2,\qquad
 \left\lvert t_l(\xi)\right\rvert\le C L_j M^{-c(j-l)}\quad(l<j).
 \label{loc:varied-t}$$ The quotient $$E=\frac{t_{j-1}}{t_j}=\frac{Dq_{j-1}}{Dq_j}
 \quad\hbox{satisfies}\quad
 \left\lvert D^p E\right\rvert\le C_p M^{-c}\quad(0\le p\le4).
 \label{loc:quotient}$$ Indeed the denominator is bounded away from zero. Every derivative of its reciprocal of order at most four is bounded by the already proved bounds through $D^5q_j$. Every derivative of the numerator through order four contains a derivative of $q_{j-1}$ and has a factor at most $CM^{-c}$. The product rule proves <a href="#loc:quotient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:quotient">[loc:quotient]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### A fourth derivative bound for the boundary value

Fourth-order control makes the boundary value replaceable by a cubic polynomial with a small remainder. The remainder must remain negligible even after normalizing the first nonzero correction when the two reciprocal-cosine terms nearly cancel.

<a id="loc:boundary-fourth"></a>
Let $R_j$ be the initial value at site $1$ of the solution $U$ with $U_0=1$ and $U_j=0$. This solution exists throughout our interval because $t_j$ does not vanish. We claim that, with $\rho=4/5$, $$L_j^2\left\lvert D^4R_j\right\rvert\le C M^{-2-\rho}.
 \label{loc:boundary-fourth}$$

Consider the $(j-1)$-by-$(j-1)$ tridiagonal matrix $B$ with diagonal $v(q_a)$, $1\le a\le j-1$, and both adjacent diagonals equal to $-1$. Its determinant is $t_j$: expansion along the last row gives the same recursion and initial conditions as those for $t$. The interior boundary problem is $BU=e_1$, so $R_j=(B^{-1})_{11}$. More explicitly, its inverse $G=B^{-1}$ is <a id="loc:green"></a>$$G_{ab}=t_{\min(a,b)}U_{\max(a,b)}.
 \label{loc:green}$$ To check this formula, fix column $b$. Its entries solve the homogeneous recurrence except at row $b$, and are zero at the boundary sites $0,j$. At row $b$ the output of $B$ is $t_{b+1}U_b-t_bU_{b+1}=1$, by the constant Wronskian and the data $t_0=0,t_1=1,U_0=1$. Thus the column is precisely $B^{-1}e_b$.

At the right boundary the same Wronskian gives $U_{j-1}=1/t_j$. Backward propagation and <a href="#loc:varied-t" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:varied-t">[loc:varied-t]</a> yield <a id="loc:boundary-backwards"></a>$$L_j\left\lvert U_a\right\rvert\le2M^{j-a-1}.
 \label{loc:boundary-backwards}$$ For $d_a=j-a\ge1$, [loc:green,loc:varied-t,loc:boundary-backwards] [loc:green](#loc:green), [loc:varied-t](#loc:varied-t), [loc:boundary-backwards](#loc:boundary-backwards) therefore give <a id="loc:green-size"></a>$$\left\lvert G_{ab}\right\rvert\le C
 M^{\min(d_a,d_b)-1-c\max(d_a,d_b)}.
 \label{loc:green-size}$$ The chain rule and the bounds for derivatives of $q_a$ also give <a id="loc:potential-derivatives"></a>$$\left\lvert D^p v(q_a)\right\rvert\le C_p M^{1-cp d_a}
 \quad(1\le p\le4).
 \label{loc:potential-derivatives}$$ In fact every term contains a derivative of $v$ bounded by $C_pM$ and derivatives of $q_a$ whose total order is $p$.

<a id="loc:inverse-chains"></a>
Starting with $DG=-G(DB)G$, differentiate three more times and take the $(1,1)$ entry. The result is a finite linear combination, with fixed integer coefficients, of sums of terms $$U_{a_1}(D^{p_1}v_{a_1})G_{a_1a_2}
 (D^{p_2}v_{a_2})\cdots
 G_{a_{s-1}a_s}(D^{p_s}v_{a_s})U_{a_s},
 \qquad p_i\ge1,\quad \sum_{i=1}^{s}p_i=4.
 \label{loc:inverse-chains}$$ Here $v_a=v(q_a)$, $1\le s\le4$, and each site index is summed independently from $1$ to $j-1$. This form follows inductively: differentiating a potential factor increases its derivative order, whereas differentiating any inverse factor inserts another $-G(DB)G$. Also $G_{1a}=U_a$ because $t_1=1$.

Put $d_i=j-a_i$. After multiplication by $L_j^2$, the exponent of $M$ bounding a term of <a href="#loc:inverse-chains" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:inverse-chains">[loc:inverse-chains]</a> is $$\begin{aligned}
 H&=-1+d_1+d_s+
 \sum_{i=1}^{s-1}\bigl(\min(d_i,d_{i+1})-c\max(d_i,d_{i+1})\bigr)
       -c\sum_{i=1}^{s}p_id_i\\
 &\le -1+2\min_i d_i-(3c-2)\sum_{i=1}^{s}p_id_i\\
 &\le -1-\frac{47}{100}\sum_{i=1}^{s}p_id_i.
\end{aligned}$$ For the first inequality, write each adjacency term as $-\left\lvert d_i-d_{i+1}\right\rvert+(1-c)\max(d_i,d_{i+1})$. The total variation is at least $d_1+d_s-2\min_i d_i$, while the sum of maxima is at most $2\sum_i d_i\le2\sum_i p_id_i$. For the last inequality use $4\min_i d_i\le\sum_i p_id_i$ and $c=99/100$. Summing over sites is harmless: for every composition of $4$, $$\sum_{d_1,\ldots,d_s\ge1}
 M^{-1-(47/100)\sum_i p_id_i}
 =M^{-1}\prod_{i=1}^{s}
 \frac{M^{-(47/100)p_i}}{1-M^{-(47/100)p_i}}
 \le C M^{-72/25}.$$ There are only finitely many such compositions and chain types. Since $72/25>2+4/5$, this proves <a href="#loc:boundary-fourth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:boundary-fourth">[loc:boundary-fourth]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### The cubic relation at successful points

<a id="loc:phase-taylor"></a>
<a id="loc:scaled-quotient"></a>
<a id="loc:scaled-phase-derivatives"></a>
Return to the two paths and put $L=\max(L_{j_+},L_{j_-})$, $h=L(\theta-\theta_*)$. Number the paths so that the first has the larger base scale. Write $Q_i(h)$ for their selected positions and $E_i(h)$ for the quotients in <a href="#loc:quotient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:quotient">[loc:quotient]</a>. With $b_i=Q_i'(0)$ and $d_i=Q_i''(0)$, the preceding estimates imply <a id="loc:phase-slopes"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 Q_i(h)&=z_i+b_ih+\tfrac12d_ih^2+\mathcal E_i(h),
 &\left\lVert\mathcal E_i\right\rVert_{C^1([-1,1])}
   &\le C\left\lvert b_i\right\rvert^3M^{1-3c}, \label{loc:phase-taylor}\\
 \left\lvert d_i\right\rvert&\le C\left\lvert b_i\right\rvert^2M^{1-2c},
 &\left\lvert b_1\right\rvert&=1,\quad
 \left\lvert b_2\right\rvert=r\in[M^{-3/5},1], \label{loc:phase-slopes}\\
 \left\lvert\partial_h^pE_i\right\rvert&\le C_pM^{-c}\left\lvert b_i\right\rvert^p
 &&(0\le p\le4). \label{loc:scaled-quotient}
\end{aligned}$$ The scale change is $\xi=(L_{j_i}/L)h=\left\lvert b_i\right\rvert h$. It also gives, for $2\le p\le5$, $$\left\lvert Q_i^{(p)}\right\rvert\le C_p\left\lvert b_i\right\rvert^pM^{1-pc},\qquad
 \left\lvert Q_i'\right\rvert\asymp\left\lvert b_i\right\rvert.
 \label{loc:scaled-phase-derivatives}$$

<a id="loc:cubic-relation"></a>
At a successful point there is a common solution with central value $1$, and its two outward values at site $1$ add to $v(q_e)$. Apply <a href="#can:boundary-expansion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:boundary-expansion">[can:boundary-expansion]</a> to both paths at that point. Since $\left\lvert t_{j_i}(\theta)\right\rvert=L\left\lvert Q_i'(h)\right\rvert$ and $v(Q_i)-E_i=K(\cos(2\pi Q_i)+(2-E_i)/K)$, multiplication by $KL^2$ yields $$KL^2\left(v(q_e)-\sum_{i=1}^{2}R_{j_i}(\theta)\right)
 =\sum_{i=1}^{2}
 \frac{1}{(Q_i')^2(\cos(2\pi Q_i)+(2-E_i)/K)}
 +O(r^{-2}M^{1-3c}).$$ The error bound uses <a href="#loc:scaled-phase-derivatives" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:scaled-phase-derivatives">[loc:scaled-phase-derivatives]</a> to compare the varied $t_{j_i}$ with their base sizes. Replace the left side by its cubic Taylor polynomial $P(h)$ at $0$. The contribution of each $R_{j_i}$ to the Taylor remainder is at most $$C K L^2\left\lvert b_i\right\rvert^4 L_{j_i}^{-2}M^{-2-\rho}
 =C K\left\lvert b_i\right\rvert^2M^{-2-\rho}.$$ Consequently every successful $h\in[-1,1]$ satisfies $$P(h)=\sum_{i=1}^{2}
 \frac{1}{(Q_i'(h))^2
       (\cos(2\pi Q_i(h))+(2-E_i(h))/K)}
       +O(M^{-1-\rho}+r^{-2}M^{1-3c}).
 \label{loc:cubic-relation}$$ The polynomial may depend on the configuration. Its degree is at most three. The displayed error is uniform on the successful set, and tends to zero uniformly over all configurations, since $r^{-2}M^{1-3c}\le M^{-77/100}$.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Polynomial extraction from a Hausdorff limit

Consider any sequence of configurations with $M\to\infty$ and nonempty successful sets $\mathcal S_M$. Pass to a subsequence on which their closures converge in Hausdorff distance to a nonempty compact set $F\subset[-1,1]$. We prove that $F$ has measure zero. Suppose to the contrary that it has positive measure.

We record precisely the compactness argument for the polynomials that will be used below. Suppose, after an indicated subtraction or multiplication of <a href="#loc:cubic-relation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:cubic-relation">[loc:cubic-relation]</a>, a polynomial $P_M^*$ of degree at most three agrees at successful points, up to an error tending to zero, with functions converging uniformly on every compact subinterval of $[-1,1]$ away from a fixed finite set of limiting poles in that interval. Choose four distinct points of $F$ outside those poles. Hausdorff convergence and approximation of points in the closure supply successful points converging to these four points. The values of $P_M^*$ there are bounded. The associated Vandermonde matrices converge to an invertible matrix, so all four coefficients of $P_M^*$ are bounded. Pass to a further subsequence on which the polynomials converge coefficientwise. For every point of $F$ away from the poles the same approximation argument shows equality with the limiting function. In particular, equality holds on a set of positive measure. Clearing the finitely many denominator factors used in that case gives an identity between real analytic functions on $\mathbb{R}$: the difference has an accumulation point of zeros and hence vanishes identically. Passing to further subsequences throughout does not change the fixed Hausdorff limit $F$.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Nonzero limiting slopes and opposite cosines

First suppose that $r$ has a positive limit $r_0$. Take convergent subsequences of the phases modulo integers and of the slopes. The first slope has a fixed sign after a further subsequence, so write its value as $b\in\{-1,1\}$. By [loc:phase-taylor,loc:scaled-quotient] [loc:phase-taylor](#loc:phase-taylor), [loc:scaled-quotient](#loc:scaled-quotient), the two terms on the right of <a href="#loc:cubic-relation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:cubic-relation">[loc:cubic-relation]</a> converge, away from their limiting poles, to $$\sec(2\pi(z+bh))+
 r_0^{-2}\sec(2\pi(z'+b'h)),\qquad \left\lvert b'\right\rvert=r_0.$$ Polynomial extraction and multiplication by both cosines show that the zero sets of these cosines on $\mathbb{R}$ coincide: at a zero of either cosine, the other must vanish because its coefficient is positive. Their zero sets are arithmetic progressions with respective spacings $1/(2\left\lvert b\right\rvert)$ and $1/(2\left\lvert b'\right\rvert)$, so $r_0=1$. The two cosines are therefore equal or opposite. Equality would leave twice a secant, which cannot equal a polynomial, as multiplication by the cosine and evaluation at one of its zeros shows. Only the opposite case remains.

<a id="loc:phase-difference"></a>
Reflect the second phase if necessary and translate it by an odd half-integer. The resulting phase $\widetilde Q_2$ has the same limiting affine phase as $Q_1$, while $$\cos(2\pi Q_2)=-\cos(2\pi\widetilde Q_2),\qquad
 (Q_2')^2=(\widetilde Q_2')^2.$$ By <a href="#loc:phase-taylor" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:phase-taylor">[loc:phase-taylor]</a>, $$\widetilde Q_2-Q_1=p(h)+O_{C^1}(M^{1-3c}),\qquad
 \deg p\le2,\qquad \left\lVert p\right\rVert_{\rm coeff}\longrightarrow0.
 \label{loc:phase-difference}$$ Here $\left\lVert p\right\rVert_{\rm coeff}$ is the maximum absolute value of its three coefficients. Define $$\ell=\max(\left\lVert p\right\rVert_{\rm coeff},K^{-1}).$$ Then $\ell\to0$, but every error of order $M^{1-3c}=M^{-197/100}$ is $o(\ell)$. The error in <a href="#loc:cubic-relation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:cubic-relation">[loc:cubic-relation]</a> is also $o(\ell)$ in the present case, since $r\to1$ and $\rho>0$.

We expand on a compact subinterval away from the zeros of the common limiting cosine. Put $$f(x,y)=y^{-2}\sec(2\pi x),\qquad
 A=2\pi(z_1+b_1h).$$ The denominators in <a href="#loc:cubic-relation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:cubic-relation">[loc:cubic-relation]</a> stay uniformly away from zero there for large $M$. Their shifts are $2/K+O(M^{-c}/K)$. Expanding the reciprocals, with their respective cosine signs, gives the sum of the two terms as $$\begin{aligned}
 &f(Q_1,Q_1')-f(\widetilde Q_2,\widetilde Q_2')\\
 &\quad-\frac2K\left((Q_1')^{-2}\sec^2(2\pi Q_1)
            +(\widetilde Q_2')^{-2}\sec^2(2\pi\widetilde Q_2)\right)
       +o(\ell).
\end{aligned}$$ The reciprocal expansion has error $O(K^{-2}+M^{-c}/K)=o(\ell)$. It is essential to subtract the two $f$ terms at the actual common phase before replacing that phase by an affine one. Taylor expansion of $f$, using <a href="#loc:phase-difference" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:phase-difference">[loc:phase-difference]</a>, gives $$f(Q_1,Q_1')-f(\widetilde Q_2,\widetilde Q_2')
 =-f_x(Q_1,Q_1')p-f_y(Q_1,Q_1')p'+o(\ell).$$ Indeed the quadratic error is $O(\ell^2)$ and the phase remainder is $O(M^{1-3c})$. Moreover $(Q_1,Q_1')=(z_1+b_1h,b_1)+O(M^{1-2c})$; replacing the derivatives of $f$ by their values at this affine pair produces only $O(\ell M^{1-2c})=o(\ell)$. Similarly, the preceding $K^{-1}$ term can be evaluated at the common affine pair with error $o(K^{-1})$. We conclude that <a href="#loc:cubic-relation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:cubic-relation">[loc:cubic-relation]</a> becomes <a id="loc:opposite-expansion"></a>$$P(h)=\frac{2p'}{b_1^3}\sec A
      -\frac{2\pi p}{b_1^2}\sin A\,\sec^2 A
      -\frac4{K b_1^2}\sec^2 A+o(\ell).
 \label{loc:opposite-expansion}$$

Divide by $\ell$ and take a subsequence such that $p/\ell\to\bar p$ coefficientwise and $K^{-1}/\ell\to\beta$. At least one is nonzero, because $\max(\left\lVert p/\ell\right\rVert_{\rm coeff},K^{-1}/\ell)=1$. Polynomial extraction now applies to $P/\ell$. Multiply its limiting identity by $b^2\cos^2(2\pi(z+bh))$ and evaluate at every zero of that cosine. One obtains <a id="loc:alternating-obstruction"></a>$$2\pi\bar p(h)\sin(2\pi(z+bh))+4\beta=0
 \quad\hbox{at every cosine zero.}
 \label{loc:alternating-obstruction}$$ The zeros form an unbounded arithmetic progression and their sine values alternate between $1$ and $-1$. Thus $\bar p$ is bounded on that progression and must be constant. The alternating signs then force both that constant and $\beta$ to be zero, contradicting their normalization. This excludes every positive limiting slope.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### A vanishing second slope

<a id="loc:slow-derivatives"></a>
It remains to treat $r\to0$. After a subsequence, the first term of <a href="#loc:cubic-relation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:cubic-relation">[loc:cubic-relation]</a> converges away from its poles to $\sec(2\pi(z+bh))$, with $\left\lvert b\right\rvert=1$. Write the second term as <a id="loc:slow-denominator"></a>$$g(h)=\frac1{r^2S(h)},\qquad
 S(h)=\left(\frac{Q_2'(h)}r\right)^2
       \left(\cos(2\pi Q_2(h))+\frac{2-E_2(h)}K\right),
 \qquad w=\max(\left\lvert S(0)\right\rvert,r).
 \label{loc:slow-denominator}$$ Uniformly on $[-1,1]$, $$\left\lvert S^{(m)}\right\rvert\le C_m r^m\quad(1\le m\le4).
 \label{loc:slow-derivatives}$$ To see every derivative involved, set $a=Q_2'/r$. Then $a$ is bounded and bounded away from zero, and $$\left\lvert a^{(m)}\right\rvert\le C_m r^m M^{1-(m+1)c}\le C_m r^m
 \quad(1\le m\le4)$$ by <a href="#loc:scaled-phase-derivatives" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:scaled-phase-derivatives">[loc:scaled-phase-derivatives]</a>. The derivatives through order four of the cosine factor are $O(r^m)$ by the chain rule; those of the shift are bounded by $C_mr^mM^{-c}/K$ by <a href="#loc:scaled-quotient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:scaled-quotient">[loc:scaled-quotient]</a>. The product rule proves <a href="#loc:slow-derivatives" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:slow-derivatives">[loc:slow-derivatives]</a>. This is the point requiring the fifth derivative of $Q_2$.

<a id="loc:slow-nonzero-slope"></a>
If $w\to0$, there is also the sharper fact $$\frac{\left\lvert S'(0)\right\rvert}r\longrightarrow2\pi.
 \label{loc:slow-nonzero-slope}$$ Indeed $a(0)^2=1$ and $\left\lvert Q_2'(0)\right\rvert=r$. The cosine with its shift equals $S(0)\to0$, so $\left\lvert\sin(2\pi Q_2(0))\right\rvert\to1$. Differentiating the factor $a^2$ contributes $O(rwM^{1-2c})=o(r)$; differentiating the shift contributes $O(rM^{-c}/K)=o(r)$. The remaining contribution is $-2\pi\sin(2\pi Q_2(0))Q_2'(0)$, which proves the assertion.

Take a subsequence on which $r/w$ has a limit. If this limit is positive, then $w\to0$, and Taylor’s formula gives $$\frac{S(h)}w=\frac{S(0)}w+\frac{S'(0)}w h+O(r^2/w).$$ Here $r^2/w\le r\to0$. The coefficients are bounded; by <a href="#loc:slow-nonzero-slope" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:slow-nonzero-slope">[loc:slow-nonzero-slope]</a>, a further subsequence gives a limiting affine polynomial $A_0(h)$ with nonzero slope. Multiply <a href="#loc:cubic-relation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:cubic-relation">[loc:cubic-relation]</a> by $r^2w$. Its first term and its error tend to zero locally away from the fast poles, while its second term converges to $1/A_0(h)$ away from the one possible zero of $A_0$. Polynomial extraction would therefore give a polynomial of degree at most three equal to $1/A_0$. Multiplication by $A_0$ and evaluation at its real zero is a contradiction.

<a id="loc:almost-constant-denominator"></a>
We may consequently assume $r/w\to0$. Then $w=\left\lvert S(0)\right\rvert$ eventually, and $$\frac{S(h)}{S(0)}=1+O(r/w)\longrightarrow1
 \quad\hbox{uniformly on }[-1,1].
 \label{loc:almost-constant-denominator}$$ In particular $S$ has no zeros there for large $M$. Let $T_3g$ be the cubic Taylor polynomial of $g$ at $0$. If $w$ stays bounded away from zero, the reciprocal derivative formula and <a href="#loc:slow-derivatives" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:slow-derivatives">[loc:slow-derivatives]</a> give $g^{(4)}=O(r^2)$ uniformly. Thus $g-T_3g=O(r^2)$. Subtracting $T_3g$ from <a href="#loc:cubic-relation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:cubic-relation">[loc:cubic-relation]</a> leaves only the fast secant in the limit. It cannot agree with a cubic polynomial, by multiplication by its cosine and evaluation at a zero.

<a id="loc:reciprocal-fourth"></a>
The last case is $w\to0$ and $r/w\to0$. To track also the possibly large remainder of $g$, differentiate explicitly: $$g^{(4)}=\frac1{r^2}\left(
 \frac{24(S')^4}{S^5}
 -\frac{36(S')^2S''}{S^4}
 +\frac{6(S'')^2+8S'S'''}{S^3}
 -\frac{S''''}{S^2}\right).
 \label{loc:reciprocal-fourth}$$ By [loc:slow-derivatives,loc:slow-nonzero-slope] [loc:slow-derivatives](#loc:slow-derivatives), [loc:slow-nonzero-slope](#loc:slow-nonzero-slope), $S'(h)/S'(0)=1+O(r)$ uniformly. Together with <a href="#loc:almost-constant-denominator" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:almost-constant-denominator">[loc:almost-constant-denominator]</a>, this makes the first term in <a href="#loc:reciprocal-fourth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:reciprocal-fourth">[loc:reciprocal-fourth]</a> equal to $$(24+o(1))\frac{S'(0)^4}{r^2S(0)^5}.$$ Relative to that term, the remaining three displayed contributions are respectively $O(w)$, $O(w^2)$, and $O(w^3)$: for example the first ratio is bounded by $C\left\lvert S\right\rvert\left\lvert S''\right\rvert/\left\lvert S'\right\rvert^2\le Cw$. All these comparisons are uniform on $[-1,1]$. Put $$a_M=\frac{S'(0)^4}{r^2S(0)^5}.$$ Taylor’s formula with integral remainder, valid on both sides of zero, now yields <a id="loc:quartic-remainder"></a>$$g(h)-T_3g(h)=a_Mh^4+o(\left\lvert a_M\right\rvert)
 \quad\hbox{uniformly on }[-1,1].
 \label{loc:quartic-remainder}$$ If $\left\lvert a_M\right\rvert\to\infty$, subtract $T_3g$ from <a href="#loc:cubic-relation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:cubic-relation">[loc:cubic-relation]</a> and divide by $a_M$. The fast term tends to zero away from its poles and the original error also tends to zero after this division. Polynomial extraction would identify a polynomial of degree at most three with $h^4$, which is impossible. Otherwise pass to a subsequence with $a_M\to a\in\mathbb{R}$. Without division, the same subtraction gives a limiting identity between a cubic polynomial and $$\sec(2\pi(z+bh))+ah^4.$$ Move the quartic term to the polynomial side and multiply by the cosine. Evaluation at any of its zeros again gives a contradiction. These alternatives exhaust the vanishing-slope case.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Uniform neighborhoods of successful sets

We have proved that every Hausdorff limit of nonempty closures of successful sets along $M\to\infty$ has measure zero. This implies the uniform assertion of <a href="#can:local-scarcity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:local-scarcity">8</a>, including the neighborhood width in its statement. Indeed, if the assertion failed for some $\eta>0$, then for every integer $n$ one could choose $M_n\ge n$ and a successful set $\mathcal S_n\subset[-1,1]$ such that $$\operatorname{Leb}_{\mathbb{R}}\{x:\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,\mathcal S_n)<1/n\}>\eta.$$ These sets are nonempty. Compactness of the nonempty compact subsets of $[-1,1]$ for Hausdorff distance supplies a subsequence of their closures with limit $F$. For every $\varepsilon>0$, eventually $$\{x:\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,\mathcal S_n)<1/n\}
 \subset\{x:\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,F)<\varepsilon\}.$$ Thus every $\varepsilon$-neighborhood of $F$ has measure at least $\eta$. Continuity of Lebesgue measure from above for these bounded neighborhoods implies $\operatorname{Leb}_{\mathbb{R}}(F)\ge\eta$, contrary to the result just proved. The empty successful set satisfies the claimed estimate automatically. This completes the proof of <a href="#can:local-scarcity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:local-scarcity">8</a>.

<a id="bridge:section"></a>
### A product estimate across a fast bridge

The second estimate compares observations near opposite ends of a long interval on which the transfer matrix grows almost at its maximum rate. The comparison comes from two transverse families of contracting graphs in the middle of the interval. All measures in this section are the normalized area measure, expressed in the coordinates $(q_i,q_{i-1})$ when convenient, whenever they define probabilities or expectations. Label lengths and slice integrals use ordinary one-dimensional Lebesgue measure in their displayed coordinates.

<a id="bridge:shortfalls"></a>
For distinct integers $d,e$, write $$\mathfrak h_{d,e}
   =1-\frac{\log_M\left\lVert T_{d,e}\right\rVert}{\left\lvert d-e\right\rvert}.
 \label{bridge:shortfalls}$$ These quantities belong to $[0,1]$. An observation with bound $(R,L,B)$ means a function of at most $R$ such quantities whose defining function on $[0,1]^R$ has absolute value at most $B$ and Lipschitz constant at most $L$ for the maximum norm. Allowing fewer than $R$ arguments is harmless. The defining functions and the integer indices may vary from one application to another, provided these bounds are preserved. Equivalently, these bounds may be imposed only on the realized range $\mathcal R\subseteq[0,1]^R$ of the arguments. Indeed, a function $f$ on that range extends by $u\mapsto\inf_{v\in\mathcal R}(f(v)+L\left\lVert u-v\right\rVert_\infty)$; clipping this extension to $[-B,B]$ preserves its values on $\mathcal R$ and its Lipschitz bound.

<a id="stmt-bridge-theorem"></a>
<div id="bridge:theorem" class="theorem">

<a id="bridge:product"></a>
**Theorem 9** (Product estimate across a fast bridge). *There are absolute constants $c_b\in(0,1)$, $\kappa>0$, and $\sigma>0$ with the following property. Fix observation bounds $(R,L,B)$ and a threshold $h>0$. For sufficiently large $M$ and sufficiently large $n=b-a$, let $W,H$ be observations with these bounds, with $W\geq0$. All indices occurring in one observation are within $\kappa n$ of $a$, and all indices occurring in the other are within $\kappa n$ of $b$. Either assignment of $W,H$ to the two ends is allowed. Then $$\mathbb{E}\!\left[
   \mathbf{1}_{\{\log_M\left\lVert T_{a,b}\right\rVert\geq c_b n\}}
   W\mathbf{1}_{\{H>h\}}
 \right]
 \leq
 C\bigl(\mathbb{E}W+M^{-\sigma n}\bigr)\mathbb{P}(H>h/2).
 \label{bridge:product}$$ The constants $c_b,\kappa,\sigma$ are independent of the observation bounds and threshold. The constant $C$ and the lower size requirements for $M,n$ may depend on $(R,L,B)$ and $h$, but not on the indices, the defining functions, or the bridge location.*

</div>

We first construct the graphs needed in the proof. Finite-time contracting directions and curves have been developed in stable-manifold constructions under weak hyperbolicity hypotheses <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[HollandLuzzatto2006](#ref-HollandLuzzatto2006)]. Here the graphs are level sets of a distant orbit coordinate, obtained from finite Dirichlet problems; we prove their extent and transverse distortion directly. We use $c=0.98$ throughout the construction. A direction is understood modulo sign.

<a id="stmt-bridge-graphs"></a>
<div id="bridge:graphs" class="lemma">

<a id="bridge:prefix-growth"></a>
<a id="bridge:graph-contraction"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Contracting graphs and their distortion). *For sufficiently large $M$, suppose $B_+\geq2$ and, at a fixed state with central coordinates $(x,y)=(q_0,q_{-1})$, $$D_l:=\left\lVert T_{0,l}\right\rVert\geq M^{cl}
 \qquad(1\leq l\leq B_+).
 \label{bridge:prefix-growth}$$ For at least one choice $j\in\{B_+-1,B_+\}$ there is a smooth graph through this state, parametrized by $q_{-1}=y+r$, $\left\lvert r\right\rvert\leq1/4$, on which the lifted coordinate $q_j$ is fixed. On this graph, $$\left\lvert q_p(r)-q_p(0)\right\rvert+
 \left\lvert\frac{\,\mathrm{d}q_p(r)}{\,\mathrm{d}r}\right\rvert
 \leq C M^{-0.8(p+1)}
 \qquad(0\leq p<j).
 \label{bridge:graph-contraction}$$ The corresponding endpoint pair at time $B_+$ changes by at most $C M^{-0.8(B_+-1)}$. The partial derivative $\partial q_j/\partial q_0$, with $q_{-1}$ held independent, is nonzero throughout the graph, and the absolute values of this partial derivative at any two points of the graph have a ratio between two absolute positive constants.*

<a id="bridge:row-choice"></a>
*For a fixed choice of $j$, one may require the closed eligibility condition $$\left\lvert\partial q_j/\partial q_0\right\rvert\geq D_{B_+}/3
 \quad\text{at the initial point}.
 \label{bridge:row-choice}$$ The graphs depend continuously on eligible initial points. There is an identical backward statement, with almost horizontal graphs in $(x,y)$, obtained by using $(q_{-1},q_0)$ as the ordered initial pair.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-bridge-graphs"><summary>Proof of stmt-bridge-graphs</summary>

<a id="bridge:direction-step"></a>
<a id="bridge:stable-prefix"></a>
*Proof.* Let $s_l$ be a most contracted unit input direction for $T_{0,l}$. Since a single inverse step has norm at most $M$, $$\left\lVert T_{0,l}s_{l+1}\right\rVert\leq\frac{M}{D_{l+1}}.$$ The projection onto the expanded input direction for $T_{0,l}$ therefore gives $$\left\lvert s_{l+1}\wedge s_l\right\rvert\leq\frac{M}{D_lD_{l+1}}.
 \label{bridge:direction-step}$$ The absolute sine between two unoriented lines satisfies the triangle inequality. Summing <a href="#bridge:direction-step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:direction-step">[bridge:direction-step]</a>, and using <a href="#bridge:prefix-growth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:prefix-growth">[bridge:prefix-growth]</a>, yields $$\left\lvert s_{B_+}\wedge s_l\right\rvert
 \leq C M^{1-c-2cl}
 \qquad(1\leq l\leq B_+).$$ Resolve $s_{B_+}$ into the singular input directions for $T_{0,l}$. Using $D_l\leq M^l$ as well as its lower bound gives $$\left\lVert T_{0,l}s_{B_+}\right\rVert
 \leq M^{-cl}+C M^{l+1-c-2cl}
 \leq C M^{-0.94l}.
 \label{bridge:stable-prefix}$$ The last inequality follows from $1-c+(1-2c)l=0.02-0.96l\leq-0.94l$ for $l\geq1$.

The first component of an input vector becomes the second component after one step. Thus <a href="#bridge:stable-prefix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:stable-prefix">[bridge:stable-prefix]</a> at $l=1$ shows that $s_{B_+}$ is nearly vertical. Let $t$ solve the variational recurrence with $t_{-1}=0$, $t_0=1$. Horizontal input has a projection bounded away from zero onto the expanded input direction for $T_{0,B_+}$. For large $M$, at least one of the two coordinates $t_{B_+}$ and $t_{B_+-1}$ consequently has magnitude at least $D_{B_+}/3$. Choose the corresponding $j$. This proves that the two closed choices in <a href="#bridge:row-choice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:row-choice">[bridge:row-choice]</a> cover the states under consideration.

<a id="bridge:linear-dirichlet"></a>
The row of $T_{0,B_+}$ selecting $q_j$ has norm at least $\left\lvert t_j\right\rvert\geq D_{B_+}/3$, and its value on $s_{B_+}$ has magnitude at most $D_{B_+}^{-1}$. Its kernel direction therefore differs from $s_{B_+}$ by $O(D_{B_+}^{-2})$. Normalize that direction so that its second component is 1, and let $U$ denote its variational solution. Then $U_{-1}=1$, $U_j=0$, and, for $0\leq p<j$, $$\left\lvert U_p\right\rvert\leq C M^{-0.9(p+1)}.
 \label{bridge:linear-dirichlet}$$ Indeed, apply <a href="#bridge:stable-prefix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:stable-prefix">[bridge:stable-prefix]</a> at $l=p+1$ to its main component. The error component is bounded by $$C M^{p+1}D_{B_+}^{-2}
 \leq C M^{-0.96(p+1)},$$ because $p+1\leq B_+$. Normalization changes these bounds only by an absolute factor.

<a id="bridge:green"></a>
We now solve the nonlinear boundary problem. Let $\xi_p$ be the change of the lifted position $q_p$, with boundary data $\xi_{-1}=r$ and $\xi_j=0$. The tridiagonal matrix on $0,\ldots,j-1$ with diagonal $v(q_p)$ and adjacent entries $-1$ has determinant $t_j\ne0$. Its inverse is $$G_{lp}=t_{\min(l,p)}U_{\max(l,p)}.
 \label{bridge:green}$$ For completeness, the proposed column solves the homogeneous recurrence except at its source. At that source the output is 1, because the Wronskian $t_{p+1}U_p-t_pU_{p+1}$ equals its boundary value 1. Both boundary values of the proposed column are zero. This verifies <a href="#bridge:green" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:green">[bridge:green]</a> directly.

<a id="bridge:fixed-point"></a>
Subtract the original orbit recurrence from the new one, and move the nonlinear Taylor remainder to the right side. With its sign absorbed into $F_p$, the boundary problem becomes $$\xi_l=U_l r+\sum_{p=0}^{j-1}G_{lp}F_p(\xi_p),
 \qquad
 \left\lvert F_p(u)\right\rvert\leq C M u^2,
 \quad
 \left\lvert F'_p(u)\right\rvert\leq C M\left\lvert u\right\rvert.
 \label{bridge:fixed-point}$$ Use the finite-dimensional weighted norm $$\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert_*=\max_{0\leq l<j}
          M^{0.8(l+1)}\left\lvert\xi_l\right\rvert.$$ On a ball of fixed radius $R_0$, the Lipschitz bound for the nonlinear part of <a href="#bridge:fixed-point" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:fixed-point">[bridge:fixed-point]</a>, before weighting its output at index $l$, is bounded by a constant depending on $R_0$ times $$\sum_{p\leq l}
 M^{p-0.9(l+1)+1-1.6(p+1)}
 +\sum_{p>l}
 M^{l-0.9(p+1)+1-1.6(p+1)}.$$ Here $\left\lvert t_p\right\rvert\leq M^p$ and <a href="#bridge:linear-dirichlet" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:linear-dirichlet">[bridge:linear-dirichlet]</a> were used. After multiplying by $M^{0.8(l+1)}$, the exponents are, respectively, $$-0.1(l+1)-0.6(p+1),
 \qquad
 1.8(l+1)-2.5(p+1).$$ Their sums tend uniformly to zero as $M$ tends to infinity, independently of $j$. The linear term $Ur$ has bounded weighted norm for $\left\lvert r\right\rvert\leq1/4$. Choose $R_0$ larger than twice this bound and then choose $M$ large. The right side of <a href="#bridge:fixed-point" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:fixed-point">[bridge:fixed-point]</a> maps this ball to itself and is a contraction there. Its unique fixed point gives the desired graph on the whole parameter interval.

The same estimates show that $I-G\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(F'_p(\xi_p))$ has a uniformly bounded inverse in this weighted norm. Multiplication by the original Dirichlet matrix identifies it with the varied linearized Dirichlet problem. Consequently that problem is invertible, and the finite-dimensional implicit function theorem gives smooth dependence on $r$. Differentiating <a href="#bridge:fixed-point" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:fixed-point">[bridge:fixed-point]</a> solves with right side $U$, which has bounded weighted norm. This proves <a href="#bridge:graph-contraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:graph-contraction">[bridge:graph-contraction]</a>. The contraction and its fixed point also depend continuously on the eligible initial data: all the coefficients are continuous there, and the contraction constant and ball are uniform. This proves the asserted continuous dependence.

If $j=B_+$, the endpoint pair estimate follows directly from the fixed boundary value and <a href="#bridge:graph-contraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:graph-contraction">[bridge:graph-contraction]</a>. If $j=B_+-1$, the recurrence and the fixed value of $q_{B_+-1}$ give $\xi_{B_+}=-\xi_{B_+-2}$, proving the same estimate without an extra factor of $M$.

<a id="bridge:determinant-distortion"></a>
It remains to control transverse distortion. At the varied orbit let $t_p(r)$ again have initial values $t_{-1}(r)=0$, $t_0(r)=1$. Invertibility of the varied Dirichlet problem gives $t_j(r)\ne0$. The graph derivative $U_p(r)=\,\mathrm{d}q_p(r)/\,\mathrm{d}r$ has boundary values 1 and 0 at indices $-1,j$ and solves that varied problem. Its Green diagonal is therefore $t_p(r)U_p(r)$. Differentiating the determinant of the varied Dirichlet matrix gives the exact identity $$\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}r}\ln\left\lvert t_j(r)\right\rvert
 =\sum_{p=0}^{j-1}t_p(r)U_p(r)^2\phi''(q_p(r)).
 \label{bridge:determinant-distortion}$$ The derivative of a diagonal entry is $\phi''(q_p(r))U_p(r)$, explaining the second factor of $U_p$. Since $\left\lvert t_p(r)\right\rvert\leq M^p$ for every varied orbit, <a href="#bridge:graph-contraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:graph-contraction">[bridge:graph-contraction]</a> bounds the absolute value of <a href="#bridge:determinant-distortion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:determinant-distortion">[bridge:determinant-distortion]</a> by $$C\sum_{p\geq0}M^p M^{-1.6(p+1)}M
 =C\sum_{p\geq0}M^{-0.6(p+1)}\leq C.$$ Integration over $\left\lvert r\right\rvert\leq1/4$ proves the bounded-ratio claim.

For backward evolution set $\widetilde q_p=q_{-1-p}$. The recurrence has the same form in these variables. At step $l$ its ordered pair is the swapped original pair at time $-l$, and the initial pair is $(q_{-1},q_0)$. Swapping coordinates is an isometry, so the backward growth hypotheses and all norm estimates are identical. The resulting graphs vary $q_0$ and have small slope in the $q_{-1}$ coordinate. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-bridge-graphs-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-bridge-graphs</summary>

<a id="bridge:middle-eligibility"></a>
*Proof of <a href="#bridge:theorem" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:theorem">9</a>.* We first find many usable middle times. On the bridge event choose a unit vector realizing $\left\lVert T_{a,b}\right\rVert$ and follow its norms along the bridge. The increments of their logarithms to base $M$ are at most 1, and their total shortfall from 1 is at most $(1-c_b)n$. By <a href="#setup:shortfall-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="setup:shortfall-count">3</a>, if $c_b$ is sufficiently close to 1, at least $\eta n$ indices $i$ in the middle third, for an absolute $\eta>0$, satisfy $$\begin{aligned}
 \left\lVert T_{i,i+l}\right\rVert&\geq M^{cl}
       &&(1\leq l\leq b-i),\\
 \left\lVert T_{i,i-l}\right\rVert&\geq M^{cl}
       &&(1\leq l\leq i-a).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{bridge:middle-eligibility}$$ In detail, remove indices admitting a forward interval starting there or a forward interval ending there whose average log increment is less than $c$. The counting bound removes at most $C(1-c_b)n/(1-c)$ indices. At a retained index, the chosen vector bounds the forward operator norm on either kind of interval from below. On an interval ending at $i$, use also equality of the forward and inverse operator norms to obtain the second inequality in <a href="#bridge:middle-eligibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:middle-eligibility">[bridge:middle-eligibility]</a>. For large $n$, the integer rounding of the middle third does not affect a fixed positive proportion.

It suffices to establish <a href="#bridge:product" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:product">[bridge:product]</a>, with a uniform constant, when the bridge indicator is replaced by the indicator of <a href="#bridge:middle-eligibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:middle-eligibility">[bridge:middle-eligibility]</a> for one fixed middle index $i$. Indeed, the bridge indicator is at most $(\eta n)^{-1}$ times the sum of these middle-index indicators; multiplying by the nonnegative weight and summing then costs only an absolute constant.

Shift $i$ to time 0. Put $B_+=b-i$ and $B_-=i-a$; both are at least $n/3-O(1)$. Apply <a href="#bridge:graphs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:graphs">10</a> forward and backward. There are two possible endpoint-coordinate choices in each direction. We work with one fixed pair of choices and eventually sum over the four possibilities. Write $Q(x,y)$ for the lifted forward coordinate held fixed, and $F(x,y)$ for the lifted backward coordinate held fixed. On forward graphs $Q_x\ne0$; on backward graphs $F_y\ne0$. For large $M$, all forward slopes $\left\lvert\,\mathrm{d}x/\,\mathrm{d}y\right\rvert$ and backward slopes $\left\lvert\,\mathrm{d}y/\,\mathrm{d}x\right\rvert$ are less than a common absolute number $a_0<1/2$. The relevant transverse partial derivatives have uniformly bounded absolute ratios along each graph.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
###### Transport of endpoint observations.

Along a forward graph the endpoint pair at time $b$ changes by at most $CM^{-0.8(B_+-1)}$. A single forward or inverse map is $M$-Lipschitz on the lifted coordinates. Thus pair differences throughout the window of radius $\kappa n$ about $b$ are at most $$C M^{\kappa n+O(1)}M^{-0.8(B_+-1)}.$$ The one-step matrix difference is at most $CM$ times the position difference. Since forward and inverse product norms agree, the endpoints of each observation interval may first be put in increasing order. For a product of length at most $2\kappa n$, the telescoping product identity therefore bounds its matrix difference by a length factor times $$C M^{3\kappa n+O(1)}M^{-0.8(B_+-1)}.$$ Every product norm compared here is at least 1. The elementary inequalities $\left\lvert\left\lVert A\right\rVert-\left\lVert B\right\rVert\right\rvert\leq\left\lVert A-B\right\rVert$ and $\left\lvert\log u-\log v\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert u-v\right\rvert$ for $u,v\geq1$ consequently give the same exponential control for the normalized logarithms in <a href="#bridge:shortfalls" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:shortfalls">[bridge:shortfalls]</a>. The positive integer interval lengths in their denominators cause no additional loss.

<a id="bridge:observation-error"></a>
Choose fixed $\kappa,\sigma>0$ with <a id="bridge:error-exponents"></a>$$3\kappa+\sigma<0.8/3.
 \label{bridge:error-exponents}$$ The strict margin absorbs the length factor and the bounded powers of $M$ when $n$ is large. After increasing the size requirements to account for the observation bounds, each observation at the forward end changes by at most $$\delta_n=C M^{-\sigma n}
 \label{bridge:observation-error}$$ along a forward graph. The same statement holds for an observation at the backward end along a backward graph. All these estimates are uniform in the chosen middle index, endpoint-coordinate choices, and observation indices. Increase $M,n$ further so that $\delta_n<h/2$.

shows the two transverse coordinate families used next. It depicts the change of variables, not the motion of an orbit.

<figcaption>Coordinate schematic for the bridge change of variables, not an orbit plot. A forward level is labelled at $y = y_{c}$ by $s$; the eligible slice $D_{s}$ is swept across the square along transverse backward levels to the coordinate $(u,Θ_{s}(u,y))$.</figcaption>
</figure>
