```latex
  \node[below] at (5.4,0) {$u$};
  \draw[sweeporange,thick]
    (0.2,1.17) .. controls (2.5,1.26) and (4.6,1.43) .. (6.8,1.51);
  \draw[sweeporange,thick]
    (0.2,2.02) .. controls (2.5,2.13) and (4.6,2.40) .. (6.8,2.46);
  \draw[sweeporange,thick]
    (0.2,2.91) .. controls (2.5,3.02) and (4.6,3.16) .. (6.8,3.24);
  \draw[leafblue,very thick]
    (3.0,0.18) .. controls (2.86,1.35) and (3.18,2.69) .. (3.06,4.33);
  \fill[leafblue] (3.07,2.25) circle (1.7pt);
  \node[below right,leafblue] at (3.07,2.25) {$s$};
  \draw[leafblue,thick,{Bar[width=4pt]}-{Bar[width=4pt]}]
    (2.95,1.34) -- (3.13,3.11);
  \node[left,leafblue,fill=white,inner sep=1pt] at (2.75,3.13) {$D_s$};
  \fill[sweeporange] (5.4,2.40) circle (1.7pt);
  \node[right,sweeporange,fill=white,inner sep=1pt]
    at (5.4,2.40) {$\Theta_s(u,y)$};
  \node[above,leafblue] at (3.18,4.32) {forward $Q$-level};
  \node[above,sweeporange] at (5.74,3.25) {backward $F$-levels};
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 24 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A coordinate diagram in a square region with axes $x$ (horizontal) and $y$ (vertical). A horizontal level is marked at $y_c$ on the vertical axis. A vertical line intersects this level at coordinate $s$, labeled "forward $Q$-level", with a vertical segment marked $D_s$. Multiple curves transverse to the vertical line are labeled "backward $F$-levels". A point on one of these backward levels is marked and labeled $\Theta_s(u, y)$, corresponding to horizontal coordinate $u$.
```


<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
###### Forward labels and their area.

Assume first that $H$ is at the forward end and $W$ at the backward end. Cover the middle-time torus by the finitely many closed squares of a grid of side $s_0=1/32$ in a fundamental domain. Overlaps on boundaries have area zero and cause no problem for an upper bound. Fix one square $I_x\times I_y$ and choose $y_c\in I_y$. All position functions in this square and its small neighborhood are computed from the same real lifted recurrence starting at $(x,y)$.

Every forward graph through an eligible point in the square extends over all of $I_y$, with a margin, since its parameter range has length $1/2$ and $s_0<1/4$. Label it by its intercept $s$ at $y=y_c$ and write it as $(X(s,y),y)$. Two such graphs which meet have the same value of $Q$ and coincide locally because $Q_x\ne0$. They then coincide on the whole common interval: the coincidence set is both open, by implicit uniqueness, and closed, by continuity. In particular distinct labels have disjoint images at every fixed $y$.

Let $E$ be the labels of forward graphs through forward-eligible points of this square for which $H\geq h$. We use closed eligibility conditions, including <a href="#bridge:row-choice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:row-choice">[bridge:row-choice]</a>. They and $H\geq h$ define a compact subset of the closed square. Continuous dependence of the graph in <a href="#bridge:graphs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:graphs">10</a> makes its intercept a continuous function of the initial point. Thus $E$ is compact. The graph is uniquely determined by its intercept, so these constructions define $X$ unambiguously on $E\times I_y$.

<a id="bridge:label-jacobian"></a>
For these labels, every point of the graph over $I_y$ has $H>h/2$ by <a href="#bridge:observation-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:observation-error">[bridge:observation-error]</a>. The identity $$Q(X(s,y),y)=Q(s,y_c)$$ and implicit differentiation give $$X_s(s,y)=\frac{Q_x(s,y_c)}{Q_x(X(s,y),y)}.
 \label{bridge:label-jacobian}$$ The derivative in <a href="#bridge:label-jacobian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:label-jacobian">[bridge:label-jacobian]</a> is interpreted through a local smooth extension when $E$ has empty interior; the existence of that extension is justified below. The distortion assertion of <a href="#bridge:graphs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:graphs">10</a> bounds its absolute value above and below by positive constants.

The map $(s,y)\mapsto(X(s,y),y)$ on $E\times I_y$ is injective and has Jacobian comparable to 1. Its image stays in a fixed bounded lift: the small slope bounds the displacement from the square by an absolute constant. Projection of this bounded lift to the torus has bounded multiplicity. Change of variables therefore yields <a id="bridge:label-area"></a>$$\left\lvert E\right\rvert\leq C\mathbb{P}(H>h/2).
 \label{bridge:label-area}$$ The factor $\left\lvert I_y\right\rvert=s_0$ has been absorbed into the constant.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
###### A transverse sweep for the weight.

Fix $s\in E$. Let $D_s\subseteq I_y$ consist of the parameters $y$ for which $z(y)=(X(s,y),y)$ lies in the square and is backward-eligible in the fixed backward endpoint choice. This is a compact set. Carry each $z(y)$ along its backward graph to first coordinate $u\in I_x$, and denote the resulting second coordinate by $\Theta_s(u,y)$. The carry is defined throughout $I_x$, with a margin.

For each fixed $u$, the map $y\mapsto\Theta_s(u,y)$ is injective on $D_s$. If two carried points agreed, their backward graphs would coincide throughout $I_x$ by regular-level uniqueness. A single such graph cannot meet the chosen forward graph in two distinct points: two intersections would satisfy both $\left\lvert\Delta x\right\rvert\leq a_0\left\lvert\Delta y\right\rvert$ and $\left\lvert\Delta y\right\rvert\leq a_0\left\lvert\Delta x\right\rvert$, forcing equality of the points. This proves the asserted injectivity.

<a id="bridge:sweep-jacobian"></a>
The equation defining the carry is $$F(u,\Theta_s(u,y))=F(X(s,y),y).$$ Differentiation with respect to the starting parameter gives $$\partial_y\Theta_s(u,y)
 =\frac{F_y(z(y))+F_x(z(y))X_y(s,y)}
        {F_y(u,\Theta_s(u,y))}.
 \label{bridge:sweep-jacobian}$$ The ratio of the two $F_y$ terms is uniformly bounded above and below along the backward graph. Moreover $\left\lvert F_x/F_y\right\rvert\leq a_0$ at its starting point, since $-F_x/F_y$ is its slope, and $\left\lvert X_y\right\rvert\leq a_0$. Hence the absolute value of <a href="#bridge:sweep-jacobian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:sweep-jacobian">[bridge:sweep-jacobian]</a> is uniformly bounded above and below by positive constants.

The endpoint observation estimate gives, for all $u\in I_x$ and $y\in D_s$, $$W(X(s,y),y)\leq W(u,\Theta_s(u,y))+\delta_n.$$ Apply change of variables to the injective map $(u,y)\mapsto(u,\Theta_s(u,y))$ on $I_x\times D_s$. Its image lies in a bounded lift. Since $W\geq0$, its integral over that image is at most a fixed multiple of $\mathbb{E}W$. Using <a href="#bridge:sweep-jacobian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:sweep-jacobian">[bridge:sweep-jacobian]</a> and then dividing by $\left\lvert I_x\right\rvert$ gives <a id="bridge:conditional-weight"></a>$$\int_{D_s}W(X(s,y),y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y
 \leq C\bigl(\mathbb{E}W+M^{-\sigma n}\bigr).
 \label{bridge:conditional-weight}$$

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
###### Changes of variables on the eligible subsets.

Here are details ensuring that the preceding changes of variables do not require the eligible sets to have interior. Consider first one forward graph with label $s$. It is a compact regular level arc over $I_y$ and extends with a margin beyond this interval. The partial derivative $Q_x$ is nonzero on the arc. The implicit function theorem in finitely many overlapping charts along the arc therefore continues it smoothly for intercepts in an open neighborhood of $s$. The continued level is prescribed by $Q(s',y_c)$ for each nearby intercept $s'$. Uniqueness in the overlapping charts makes these local continuations consistent. Every nearby eligible label follows the same continuation, because its regular graph passes through that intercept. Thus $X$ on $E\times I_y$ is locally the restriction of a smooth function of $(s,y)$, and <a href="#bridge:label-jacobian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:label-jacobian">[bridge:label-jacobian]</a> holds at all required points.

For the transverse sweep, fix $s$ and a point of $D_s$. Its backward graph is likewise a compact regular arc, with $F_y\ne0$, extending with margin over $I_x$. The same continuation argument applies to the smoothly varying starting points $(X(s,y),y)$ on the fixed forward graph. It gives a local smooth extension of $\Theta_s(u,y)$ in both variables, agreeing with all nearby eligible carries by uniqueness. The maps used in both area comparisons are consequently restrictions of local smooth diffeomorphisms, with the displayed nonvanishing Jacobians.

A countable cover by such charts can be split into disjoint Borel pieces. On each piece ordinary change of variables applies to its measurable subset; the images of the pieces are disjoint because the full restricted maps are injective. Summing proves the formulas on the compact eligible domains. This also establishes the measurability of all integrands and swept regions used above. No extension of eligibility to a neighborhood is assumed.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
###### Completion of the area comparison.

Every point of the fixed square satisfying both eligibility conditions and $H>h$ belongs to a forward graph with label in $E$. Change variables from $x$ to this label at each $y$, using <a href="#bridge:label-jacobian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:label-jacobian">[bridge:label-jacobian]</a>. Drop conditions other than square membership and backward eligibility, which is allowed because the weight is nonnegative. The desired weighted integral over this square and this pair of choices is bounded by $$C\int_E\int_{D_s}W(X(s,y),y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}s.$$ Combining [bridge:conditional-weight,bridge:label-area] [bridge:conditional-weight](#bridge:conditional-weight), [bridge:label-area](#bridge:label-area) bounds this by the right side of <a href="#bridge:product" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:product">[bridge:product]</a>.

If $H$ is at the backward end, begin instead with the labels of the almost horizontal backward graphs and sweep them in the $x$ direction to bound their label measure by $\mathbb{P}(H>h/2)$. For each such graph, transport its eligible points along the almost vertical forward graphs to estimate $W$. The proof just given applies with $x,y$ and $Q,F$ interchanged: the graph extents, distortion bounds, small-slope injectivity argument, and local chart construction are the same. This proves the estimate for the other assignment of the two observations as well.

Finally sum over the finitely many squares and the at most four endpoint-coordinate choices. The constants remain uniform. Average over the usable middle indices as explained after <a href="#bridge:middle-eligibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:middle-eligibility">[bridge:middle-eligibility]</a>. This establishes <a href="#bridge:product" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:product">[bridge:product]</a> and completes the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:scales"></a>
### Product distances and measures across scales

<a id="scale:long-deficit"></a>
Suppose, toward a contradiction, that there is a sequence of parameters $k\to+\infty$ for which the top Lyapunov exponent vanishes almost everywhere. All limits in this section are taken along this sequence, passing to subsequences when indicated, except where a fixed-parameter limit in orbit length is explicitly specified. The structural constants in [can:theorem,bridge:theorem] [can:theorem](#can:theorem), [bridge:theorem](#bridge:theorem) are fixed throughout; the bridge estimate’s auxiliary constants may depend on its observation bounds. Use the growth semimetric $g$ and expected shortfall $e_n$ from <a href="#scale:distance-deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:distance-deficit">[scale:distance-deficit]</a>; their parameter dependence remains suppressed. At each parameter in the sequence, the zero-exponent assumption and bounded convergence give $$\label{scale:long-deficit}
e_n\longrightarrow1\qquad(n\to\infty).$$ By contrast, the shortfall at every fixed length tends to zero as the parameter grows. We will select a dyadic range in which the shortfall first increases, and sum the loss between each whole interval and its two halves over that range. This sum telescopes, but its limiting mass must still be controlled near arrays with one constant growth rate. The local tests and measures below are designed to retain that mass.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Four-point comparison and truncation

<a id="stmt-scale-fourpoint"></a>
<div id="scale:fourpoint" class="lemma">

<a id="scale:R-fourpoint"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Four-point comparison). *Fix a base index $i$ and write $$D_j=\left\lVert T_{i,j}\right\rVert,\qquad
 R_{jp}=M^{g(j,p)-g(i,j)-g(i,p)}.$$ There is an absolute constant $C_0\ge1$ such that, for every orbit, every sufficiently large parameter, and every choice of integer indices, $$\label{scale:R-fourpoint}
 R_{jp}\le C_0\max\{R_{jl},R_{lp}\}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-scale-fourpoint"><summary>Proof of stmt-scale-fourpoint</summary>

<a id="scale:R-comparison"></a>
*Proof.* Choose a most expanded unit input direction $a_j$ for $T_{i,j}$; if $D_j=1$, any singular direction may be used. Set $$Q_{jp}=\max\{|a_j\wedge a_p|,D_j^{-2},D_p^{-2}\}.$$ We claim $$\label{scale:R-comparison}
 Q_{jp}\le R_{jp}\le3Q_{jp}.$$ To obtain the sine lower bound, use a unit input at the base perpendicular to $a_p$. Its norm at $p$ is $D_p^{-1}$, whereas its norm at $j$ is at least $D_j|a_j\wedge a_p|$. The transfer from $p$ to $j$ therefore has norm at least $D_jD_p|a_j\wedge a_p|$. The inequalities $$\left\lVert T_{p,j}\right\rVert\ge D_j/D_p,\qquad
 \left\lVert T_{p,j}\right\rVert\ge D_p/D_j$$ give the other two lower bounds; the second uses equality of the norms of a determinant-one matrix and its inverse.

For the upper bound, transport a unit vector from $p$ back to the base. Its components along $a_p$ and $a_p^\perp$ have absolute values at most $D_p^{-1}$ and $D_p$, respectively. Also $$\left\lVert T_{i,j}a_p^\perp\right\rVert
 \le D_j|a_j\wedge a_p|+D_j^{-1}.$$ It follows that $$R_{jp}\le D_p^{-2}+|a_j\wedge a_p|+D_j^{-2},$$ proving <a href="#scale:R-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:R-comparison">[scale:R-comparison]</a>. By <a href="#setup:sine-triangle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="setup:sine-triangle">[setup:sine-triangle]</a>, $Q_{jp}\le2\max\{Q_{jl},Q_{lp}\}$, so $C_0=6$ is permissible. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-scale-truncation"></a>
<div id="scale:truncation" class="lemma">

<a id="scale:cut-R"></a>
<a id="scale:cut-loss"></a>
<a id="scale:cut-full"></a>
**Lemma 12** (Truncation of a cancellation). *Fix integers $i$, $n\ge1$, and $1\le s\le n$, and put $$L=i-n,\quad R=i+n,\quad L'=i-s,\quad R'=i+s,$$ $$D=n-\min\{g(i,L),g(i,R)\},\qquad
 \ell=g(i,L)+g(i,R)-g(L,R).$$ Use the quantities $R_{uv}$ of <a href="#scale:fourpoint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:fourpoint">11</a> with base $i$. Then <a id="scale:cut-wings"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 g(i,L'),g(i,R')&\ge s-D,
 \label{scale:cut-wings}\\
 R_{L'R'}&\le C_0^2\max\{R_{LR},M^{-2s+2D}\}.
 \label{scale:cut-R}
\end{aligned}$$ There is a constant $A$ independent of the parameter and indices such that the cut cancellation loss satisfies $$\label{scale:cut-loss}
 g(i,L')+g(i,R')-g(L',R')
 \ge\min\{\ell,2s-2D\}-A,$$ and consequently $$\label{scale:cut-full}
 g(L',R')\le2s-\min\{\ell,2s-2D\}+A.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-scale-truncation"><summary>Proof of stmt-scale-truncation</summary>

*Proof.* The triangle inequality and <a href="#scale:distance-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:distance-bound">[scale:distance-bound]</a> give $$g(i,L')\ge g(i,L)-g(L,L')\ge(n-D)-(n-s)=s-D,$$ and similarly on the right. In particular, $$R_{LL'}\le M^{(n-s)-(n-D)-(s-D)}=M^{-2s+2D},
 \qquad R_{RR'}\le M^{-2s+2D}.$$ Apply <a href="#scale:R-fourpoint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:R-fourpoint">[scale:R-fourpoint]</a> twice along the chain $L',L,R,R'$ to obtain <a href="#scale:cut-R" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:cut-R">[scale:cut-R]</a>. Taking negative base-$M$ logarithms gives <a href="#scale:cut-loss" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:cut-loss">[scale:cut-loss]</a>, with any fixed upper bound for $2\log_M C_0$ as $A$. Such a bound is uniform after imposing, for example, $M\ge2$. Finally, each cut wing has length at most $s$, which yields <a href="#scale:cut-full" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:cut-full">[scale:cut-full]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-scale-doubling"></a>
<div id="scale:doubling" class="lemma">

**Lemma 13** (Doubling bound). *For all sufficiently large parameters and every integer $n\ge1$, <a id="scale:doubling-equation"></a>$$\label{scale:doubling-equation}
 e_n\le e_{2n}\le C e_n+\frac{C}{n},$$ with a constant independent of $n$ and the parameter.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-scale-doubling"><summary>Proof of stmt-scale-doubling</summary>

<a id="scale:mean-cancellation"></a>
*Proof.* Use the notation of <a href="#scale:truncation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:truncation">12</a>. Both $D$ and $\ell$ are nonnegative, and $\ell\le2n$. Let $\delta>0$ be the fixed constant in <a href="#can:theorem" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:theorem">4</a>. Choose a fixed $H$ sufficiently large that $$\ell>H(1+D),\qquad s=\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 s\ge1,\quad 2D+A\le\delta s.$$ This is possible since $s\ge\ell/2-1$. Also $s\le n$ and $2s\le\ell$. Thus [scale:cut-wings,scale:cut-full] [scale:cut-wings](#scale:cut-wings), [scale:cut-full](#scale:cut-full) imply $$\min\{g(i,i-s),g(i,i+s)\}\ge(1-\delta)s,
 \qquad g(i-s,i+s)\le2D+A\le\delta s.$$ This is the event bounded by <a href="#can:theorem" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:theorem">4</a>, with spacing $s$. On the part where this integer $s$ is selected, $\ell<2s+2$. Writing the constants of that theorem as $C_{\rm can},\gamma>0$, we obtain $$\mathbb{E}\bigl[\ell\,\mathbf{1}_{\{\ell>H(1+D)\}}\bigr]
 \le C_{\rm can}\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}(2s+2)e^{-\gamma s}
 \le C.$$ The complementary contribution is at most $H(1+\mathbb{E}D)$, hence $$\label{scale:mean-cancellation}
 \mathbb{E}\ell\le C(1+\mathbb{E}D).$$ Stationarity gives $$\mathbb{E}D\le\mathbb{E}[n-g(i,L)]+\mathbb{E}[n-g(i,R)]=2ne_n,
 \qquad e_{2n}=e_n+\frac{\mathbb{E}\ell}{2n}.$$ These identities and <a href="#scale:mean-cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:mean-cancellation">[scale:mean-cancellation]</a> prove the result. In particular the lower inequality uses only $\ell\ge0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### Selecting the first growing deficit

<a id="stmt-scale-selection"></a>
<div id="scale:selection" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14** (Critical scales). *Along the assumed zero-exponent parameter sequence, there are powers of two $n_0=n_0(K)$ and $N=N(K)>n_0$, and numbers $\epsilon=e_N$, such that <a id="scale:selected-limits"></a>$$\label{scale:selected-limits}
 n_0\to\infty,\qquad \epsilon\to0,\qquad
 \epsilon n_0\to\infty,$$ and, for every fixed integer $m\in\mathbb{Z}$ (once the lengths below are positive integers), <a id="scale:selected-boundaries"></a>$$\label{scale:selected-boundaries}
 e_{2^m n_0}=o(\epsilon),\qquad
 e_{2^m N}=O_m(\epsilon).$$ All scale sums below run over powers of two.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-scale-selection"><summary>Proof of stmt-scale-selection</summary>

*Proof.* First, for every fixed integer $a\ge1$, we have $e_a\to0$ as $K\to\infty$. To see this, fix $0<\zeta<1$. The uniform marginal law of each $q_i$ implies $$\mathbb{P}\{|v(q_i)|<M^{1-\zeta}\}\longrightarrow0.$$ Indeed $v(x)=2+K\cos(2\pi x)$, and the length of the set on which $|\cos(2\pi x)|\le(M^{1-\zeta}+2)/K$ tends to zero. A union bound therefore shows that, with probability tending to one, all $a$ coefficients in the product satisfy $|v(q_i)|\ge M^{1-\zeta}$. Start the linear recurrence with $(u_0,u_{-1})=(1,0)$. On this event, for $M$ large, induction gives $$|u_{j+1}|\ge(M^{1-\zeta}-1)|u_j|\ge|u_j|
 \quad(0\le j<a).$$ Consequently $g(0,a)/a\ge\log_M(M^{1-\zeta}-1)$ there. The deficit always lies in $[0,1]$, so $\limsup_{K\to\infty}e_a\le\zeta$. Letting $\zeta\downarrow0$ proves the claim.

Choose powers of two $n_0\to\infty$ sufficiently slowly that $e_{n_0}\to0$, by diagonal selection over the fixed lengths $1,2,4,\ldots$. Set $$\theta_K=(n_0^{-1}+e_{n_0})^{1/2}.$$ For all sufficiently large $K$, $e_{n_0}<\theta_K<1$. By <a href="#scale:long-deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:long-deficit">[scale:long-deficit]</a>, there is a first power of two $N\ge n_0$ for which $e_N>\theta_K$. It satisfies $N>n_0$ and $e_{N/2}\le\theta_K$. Applying <a href="#scale:doubling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:doubling">13</a> once gives $$\theta_K<\epsilon=e_N\le C\theta_K+C/n_0\longrightarrow0.$$ Also $\epsilon n_0\ge\theta_K n_0\ge\sqrt{n_0}\to\infty$. For fixed $m\ge0$, iteration of <a href="#scale:doubling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:doubling">13</a> yields $$e_{2^m n_0}\le C_m(e_{n_0}+n_0^{-1})
 =C_m\theta_K^2=o(\epsilon),$$ and $$e_{2^m N}\le C_m(\epsilon+N^{-1})=O_m(\epsilon).$$ For a fixed negative $m$, the lengths $2^m n_0$ and $2^mN$ are eventually positive integers. Dyadic monotonicity bounds their deficits by $e_{n_0}=o(\epsilon)$ and $e_N=\epsilon$, respectively. This proves every assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### A compact space of distance arrays

The next construction records shortfall increments as measures on rescaled distance arrays. Its output will be locally finite limits away from affine arrays, together with a dilation balance; the discarded affine part requires separate control in the final argument.

Let $\mathcal D=\mathbb{Z}[1/2]$ denote the dyadic rational numbers. Define $X$ to be the set of symmetric semimetric arrays $d:\mathcal D\times\mathcal D\to[0,\infty)$ satisfying <a id="scale:ambient-bound"></a>$$\label{scale:ambient-bound}
 d(s,t)\le|s-t|+2.$$ Give $X$ the topology of pointwise convergence. It is a closed subset of a countable product of compact intervals, and hence is compact and metrizable. Its compact subset of affine arrays is $$\mathcal A=\{d_w:0\le w\le1\},\qquad d_w(s,t)=w|t-s|.$$ These are precisely the arrays of this form that belong to $X$: the bound at arbitrarily large separations forces $w\le1$. Write $\mathcal U=X\setminus\mathcal A$.

<a id="scale:rescaled-array"></a>
For a positive integer $n$, define the random array $$\label{scale:rescaled-array}
 g_n(s,t)=\frac{g(\lfloor ns\rfloor,\lfloor nt\rfloor)}{n}
 \qquad(s,t\in\mathcal D).$$ It belongs to $X$, since its distances are at most $|s-t|+1/n$. Introduce the finite measures <a id="scale:prelimit-measures"></a>$$\label{scale:prelimit-measures}
 \mu_K=\epsilon^{-1}\sum_{\substack{n_0\le n<N\\n\text{ a power of }2}}
                  \mathop{\mathrm{Law}}(g_n),\qquad
 \nu_K=\epsilon^{-1}\mathop{\mathrm{Law}}(g_N),\qquad
 \xi_K=\epsilon^{-1}\mathop{\mathrm{Law}}(g_{n_0}).$$ Their total masses need not remain bounded. We will obtain limits on $\mathcal U$ by controlling local departures from affinity.

For an interval $I=[s,t]$ with distinct dyadic endpoints, let $I_l,I_r$ be its left and right halves and define continuous functions on $X$ by <a id="scale:tests"></a>$$\label{scale:tests}
 h(I)=1-\frac{d(s,t)}{t-s},\qquad
 J(I)=h(I)-\frac{h(I_l)+h(I_r)}{2},\qquad
 V(I)=\bigl(h(I_l)-h(I_r)\bigr)^2.$$ The functions $J(I)$ and $V(I)$ are nonnegative on all of $X$. The first assertion is the triangle inequality. The shortfall $h(I)$ itself need not be nonnegative on all of $X$. It does belong to $[0,1]$ on a sample $g_n$ whenever the endpoints multiplied by $n$ are integers. For any fixed dyadic interval, this alignment holds at every scale $n\ge n_0$ occurring here once $K$ is sufficiently large.

<a id="stmt-scale-local-mass"></a>
<div id="scale:local-mass" class="lemma">

<a id="scale:sum-test-bound"></a>
<a id="scale:individual-test-bound"></a>
<a id="scale:initial-test-bound"></a>
**Lemma 15** (Bounds for local tests). *Let $I$ have dyadic endpoints and length $p=2^m$, where $m\in\mathbb{Z}$ is fixed. Then $$\label{scale:sum-test-bound}
 \int\bigl(J(I)+V(I)\bigr)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_K=O_I(1).$$ Uniformly over the individual dyadic scales $n_0\le n\le N$, $$\label{scale:individual-test-bound}
 \epsilon^{-1}\mathbb{E}\bigl[h(I)(g_n)+J(I)(g_n)+V(I)(g_n)\bigr]
 =O_I(1).$$ At the initial scale the stronger bound holds: $$\label{scale:initial-test-bound}
 \epsilon^{-1}\mathbb{E}\bigl[h(I)(g_{n_0})+J(I)(g_{n_0})
                         +V(I)(g_{n_0})\bigr]=o(1).$$ All three statements concern sufficiently large $K$, so that the indicated endpoints and half-endpoints align.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-scale-local-mass"><summary>Proof of stmt-scale-local-mass</summary>

<a id="scale:J-telescope"></a>
*Proof.* By stationarity, $$\mathbb{E}h(I)(g_n)=e_{pn},\qquad
 \mathbb{E}J(I)(g_n)=e_{pn}-e_{pn/2}.$$ Summation over consecutive dyadic scales gives the exact identity $$\label{scale:J-telescope}
 \sum_{\substack{n_0\le n<N\\n\text{ a power of }2}}
       \mathbb{E}J(I)(g_n)=e_{pN/2}-e_{pn_0/2}.$$ By <a href="#scale:selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:selection">14</a> and dyadic monotonicity, its nonnegative right side is $O_I(\epsilon)$.

Put $a=h(I_l)$, $b=h(I_r)$, and $\bar h=(a+b)/2$. At the aligned sampled arrays, $0\le\bar h\le h(I)\le1$, and therefore $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{V(I)}4
 &=\frac{a^2+b^2}{2}-\bar h^2\\
 &\le\frac{a^2+b^2}{2}-h(I)^2+2J(I).
\end{aligned}$$ Write $b_q=\mathbb{E}\bigl[(1-g(0,q)/q)^2\bigr]$ for integer $q\ge1$. By stationarity and another dyadic telescoping, $$\frac14\sum_n\mathbb{E}V(I)(g_n)
 \le b_{pn_0/2}-b_{pN/2}
      +2\bigl(e_{pN/2}-e_{pn_0/2}\bigr)
 \le b_{pn_0/2}+2e_{pN/2}.$$ Here and in this display only, the unqualified sum is over the scales in <a href="#scale:J-telescope" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:J-telescope">[scale:J-telescope]</a>. Since $0\le b_q\le e_q$, the last expression is $O_I(\epsilon)$. This proves <a href="#scale:sum-test-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:sum-test-bound">[scale:sum-test-bound]</a>.

At any single aligned scale, $J(I)\le h(I)$ and $V(I)=(a-b)^2\le a+b$. Hence the expectation in <a href="#scale:individual-test-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:individual-test-bound">[scale:individual-test-bound]</a> is at most $2e_{pn}+2e_{pn/2}$. Dyadic monotonicity bounds these terms by the corresponding terminal-scale deficits, which are $O_I(\epsilon)$ by <a href="#scale:selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:selection">14</a>. At $n=n_0$ they are $o(\epsilon)$, again by that lemma, including its consequence for negative shifts. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-scale-affinity-tests"></a>
<div id="scale:affinity-tests" class="lemma">

<a id="scale:finite-test-cover"></a>
**Lemma 16** (Detection of affinity). *As $I$ ranges over intervals with dyadic endpoints and power-of-two length, the common zero set of $J(I)+V(I)$ is exactly $\mathcal A$. Consequently, every compact subset $F\subset\mathcal U$ admits finitely many such intervals $I_1,\ldots,I_b$ and a number $c_F>0$ such that $$\label{scale:finite-test-cover}
 \sum_{a=1}^{b}\bigl(J(I_a)+V(I_a)\bigr)\ge c_F
 \quad\hbox{on }F.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-scale-affinity-tests"><summary>Proof of stmt-scale-affinity-tests</summary>

*Proof.* Every affine array annihilates these tests. Conversely, suppose they all vanish. For one of the stated intervals $I$, the equalities $J(I)=V(I)=0$ say that its two halves have the same speed as $I$ and that their distances add to the full distance. Repeating this argument shows that every edge of every dyadic subdivision of $I$ has distance $w_I$ times its length, with $w_I=d(s,t)/(t-s)$. The triangle inequality gives the same upper bound between any two subdivision nodes. Applying the triangle inequality across the full interval, using those nodes as intermediate points, gives the reverse bound, and therefore equality. Every dyadic point of $I$ is a subdivision node at some level. Apply this argument to $[-2^a,2^a]$ for arbitrarily large integers $a$. The common pair $0,1$ fixes one speed on all these intervals, so the array is globally affine. The ambient bound forces that speed to lie in $[0,1]$.

For the last assertion, each point of $F$ has a positive test. The corresponding open sets cover $F$, and a finite subcover suffices. Their sum has a positive minimum on the compact set $F$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Locally finite limits and their symmetries

The limiting inequality <a href="#scale:tree-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:tree-inequality">[scale:tree-inequality]</a> is the zero-hyperbolic Gromov-product condition: after identifying points at zero semimetric distance, $B_v(s,t)/2$ is the Gromov product based at $v$; see <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Bestvina1997](#ref-Bestvina1997), Section 2.2, Definition 2.10]. The determinant-one estimate and the passage to the limiting arrays are proved here; no real-tree representation theorem is used.

<a id="stmt-scale-limits"></a>
<div id="scale:limits" class="proposition">

<a id="scale:initial-negligible"></a>
<a id="scale:tree-inequality"></a>
<a id="scale:limiting-test-bound"></a>
<a id="scale:terminal-shortfall"></a>
**Proposition 17** (Limits away from affinity). *After passing to a subsequence, the restrictions of $\mu_K$ and $\nu_K$ to $\mathcal U$ converge vaguely to locally finite nonnegative Borel measures $\mu$ and $\nu$, respectively. Moreover, $$\label{scale:initial-negligible}
 \xi_K\longrightarrow0\quad\hbox{vaguely on }\mathcal U.$$ The measures $\mu$ and $\nu$ are supported on the closed class $X_0\subset X$ consisting of arrays satisfying $d(s,t)\le|s-t|$ and the inequalities $$\label{scale:tree-inequality}
 B_v(s,t)\ge\min\{B_v(s,p),B_v(p,t)\},\qquad
 B_v(s,t)=d(v,s)+d(v,t)-d(s,t)$$ for every $v,s,t,p\in\mathcal D$. Every member of $X_0$ extends uniquely to a continuous semimetric on $\mathbb{R}$, obeying the same inequalities at all real arguments. Both limiting measures are invariant under $$(\tau_r d)(s,t)=d(s+r,t+r)\qquad(r\in\mathcal D).$$ For every interval $I$ as in <a href="#scale:local-mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:local-mass">15</a>, $$\label{scale:limiting-test-bound}
 \int(J(I)+V(I))\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu<\infty,
 \qquad
 \int(h(I)+J(I)+V(I))\,\,\mathrm{d}\nu<\infty.$$ In particular, $$\label{scale:terminal-shortfall}
 \int h([0,1])\,\,\mathrm{d}\nu\le1.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-scale-limits"><summary>Proof of stmt-scale-limits</summary>

*Proof.* For each compact $F\subset\mathcal U$, combine <a href="#scale:finite-test-cover" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:finite-test-cover">[scale:finite-test-cover]</a> with [scale:sum-test-bound,scale:individual-test-bound] to obtain uniform bounds on $\mu_K(F)$ and $\nu_K(F)$. Using <a href="#scale:initial-test-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:initial-test-bound">[scale:initial-test-bound]</a> instead gives $\xi_K(F)=o(1)$. The latter proves <a href="#scale:initial-negligible" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:initial-negligible">[scale:initial-negligible]</a>, since a continuous compactly supported test is bounded and has compact support.

Here is an explicit way to extract the local limits. Fix a compatible metric on $X$ and let $a(d)=\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(d,\mathcal A)$. Choose a continuous nondecreasing function $\chi:[0,\infty)\to[0,1]$ equal to zero on $[0,1/2]$ and to one on $[1,\infty)$. Set $\chi_j(d)=\chi(2^j a(d))$. These functions increase to one on $\mathcal U$, have compact support there, and satisfy $\chi_{j+1}=1$ on $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\chi_j$. The finite measures $\chi_j\mu_K$ and $\chi_j\nu_K$ have bounded total masses for each $j$. Weak compactness for finite measures on compact metric spaces, followed by a diagonal extraction, gives weak limits for all of them. Their restrictions agree on the open regions where the corresponding cutoffs are one: this follows by testing against continuous functions supported in such a region, for which the prelimit integrals agree. These compatible restrictions define locally finite measures on $\mathcal U$. Every compactly supported continuous function is supported where some $\chi_j$ is one, so these are the asserted vague limits.

For completeness, the support restrictions survive despite the possibly unbounded total masses. The arrays $g_n$ satisfy $g_n(s,t)\le|s-t|+1/n$. Taking logarithms in <a href="#scale:R-fourpoint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:R-fourpoint">[scale:R-fourpoint]</a>, then dividing by $n$, shows that their version of <a href="#scale:tree-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:tree-inequality">[scale:tree-inequality]</a> has error at most $(\log_M C_0)/n$. These errors are bounded uniformly by $C/n_0$ at every sampled scale. Any fixed strict violation, by a fixed positive margin, is thus absent from every sampled array once $K$ is large. Testing on compact subsets of its open violation set shows that it has zero limiting measure. There are countably many dyadic arguments and positive rational margins, which proves the stated support assertion. For an array with $d(s,t)\le|s-t|$, the semimetric inequality gives $$|d(s,t)-d(s',t')|\le|s-s'|+|t-t'|.$$ It therefore has a unique jointly continuous extension to real arguments; all its inequalities extend by continuity.

For fixed $r\in\mathcal D$, each prelimit law is invariant under $\tau_r$ once $n_0r$ is an integer. Indeed $\lfloor n(s+r)\rfloor=\lfloor ns\rfloor+nr$, and stationarity applies simultaneously to the array. The map $\tau_r$ is a homeomorphism of $X$ preserving $\mathcal A$, so it preserves compact support in $\mathcal U$. Invariance therefore passes to the vague limits.

Finally, multiply any nonnegative test $J(I)+V(I)$ by $\chi_j$, pass to the vague limit, and use <a href="#scale:sum-test-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:sum-test-bound">[scale:sum-test-bound]</a>. Monotone convergence as $j\to\infty$ gives the first bound in <a href="#scale:limiting-test-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:limiting-test-bound">[scale:limiting-test-bound]</a>. For $\nu$ use <a href="#scale:individual-test-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:individual-test-bound">[scale:individual-test-bound]</a> in the same way. Although $h(I)$ is not nonnegative everywhere on $X$, it is nonnegative on the aligned samples and on the supported limiting class $X_0$; these facts justify the cutoff bounds and monotone convergence for that term as well. In the case $I=[0,1]$ the uncut terminal integral is exactly $e_N/\epsilon=1$, giving <a href="#scale:terminal-shortfall" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:terminal-shortfall">[scale:terminal-shortfall]</a>. ◻

</details>

Define the dilation <a id="scale:dilation"></a>$$\label{scale:dilation}
 (Sd)(s,t)=\frac{d(2s,2t)}2.$$ It is a continuous map from $X$ into $X$ and fixes every affine array. Its inverse need not map all of $X$ into $X$, but on $X_0$ it is the homeomorphism $$(S^{-1}d)(s,t)=2d(s/2,t/2).$$ Both maps preserve $X_0\cap\mathcal U$.

<a id="stmt-scale-decomposition"></a>
<div id="scale:decomposition" class="proposition">

<a id="scale:measure-balance"></a>
**Proposition 18** (Positive dilation decomposition). *On $\mathcal U$, with the measures supported on $X_0$ as above, $$\label{scale:measure-balance}
 S_*\mu=\mu+\nu.$$ There is a locally finite nonnegative measure $\mu_\infty$, invariant under $S$ and every dyadic translation, such that <a id="scale:measure-decomposition"></a>$$\label{scale:measure-decomposition}
 \mu=\mu_\infty+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(S^{-j})_*\nu,
 \qquad S_*\mu_\infty=\mu_\infty.$$ The series is a series of positive measures dominated by $\mu$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-scale-decomposition"><summary>Proof of stmt-scale-decomposition</summary>

*Proof.* The floor convention in <a href="#scale:rescaled-array" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:rescaled-array">[scale:rescaled-array]</a> gives the exact identity $Sg_n=g_{2n}$. Hence, as finite measures on $X$, <a id="scale:finite-measure-balance"></a>$$\label{scale:finite-measure-balance}
 S_*\mu_K=\mu_K+\nu_K-\xi_K.$$ To pass to the local limits, observe that $S^{-1}(\mathcal A)
=\mathcal A$: multiplication by two permutes the dyadic rationals, so $Sd=d_w$ implies $d=d_w$. If $F\subset\mathcal U$ is compact, its preimage under $S$ is closed in compact $X$ and disjoint from $\mathcal A$, hence compact in $\mathcal U$. Thus $S$ is proper on $\mathcal U$, and composition with $S$ preserves continuous compactly supported tests there. Vague convergence and <a href="#scale:initial-negligible" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:initial-negligible">[scale:initial-negligible]</a> now give <a href="#scale:measure-balance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:measure-balance">[scale:measure-balance]</a>.

We may henceforth work on $X_0\cap\mathcal U$, where $S$ is invertible. Applying its inverse to <a href="#scale:measure-balance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:measure-balance">[scale:measure-balance]</a> and iterating gives, for every $b\ge1$, $$\mu=(S^{-b})_*\mu+\sum_{j=1}^{b}(S^{-j})_*\nu.$$ The increasing positive partial sums are dominated by $\mu$. Their limit $\lambda=\sum_{j\ge1}(S^{-j})_*\nu$ is therefore a locally finite measure. On relatively compact Borel sets define $\mu_\infty=\mu-\lambda$. The definitions agree on overlaps and extend by exhaustion to a locally finite nonnegative measure; this construction avoids subtracting infinite total masses. Monotone convergence for the positive series yields $$S_*\lambda=\nu+\lambda.$$ Subtract this identity from <a href="#scale:measure-balance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:measure-balance">[scale:measure-balance]</a> on relatively compact sets to obtain $S_*\mu_\infty=\mu_\infty$.

For $r\in\mathcal D$ and $j\ge1$, the identity $$\tau_r S^{-j}=S^{-j}\tau_{r/2^j}$$ and translation invariance of $\nu$ show that every summand of $\lambda$ is invariant under $\tau_r$. The same holds for their sum and, by local subtraction from the translation-invariant measure $\mu$, for $\mu_\infty$. This proves all assertions. ◻

</details>

<a id="shape:section"></a>
### Support restrictions and the geometry of limiting distances

Throughout this section we retain the sequence of parameters, scales, and measures constructed in [scale:selection,scale:limits] [scale:selection](#scale:selection), [scale:limits](#scale:limits). In particular, all limiting arrays belong to $X_0$, and all measures are considered on $\mathcal U=X\setminus\mathcal A$. As before, $\mathcal D$ denotes the dyadic rationals. The constants $\delta$ and $c_b$ are those fixed in [can:theorem,bridge:theorem]; neither depends on the scales or the parameter sequence.

For $d\in X_0$, let $P_d$ be the set of times having no open neighborhood on which $d(x,y)=w|x-y|$ for some constant $w$, and let $Q_d$ be the set of times having no open neighborhood on which $d(x,y)=|x-y|$. These sets are closed, and $P_d\subset Q_d$. Here and below a property of the distance on an interval concerns every pair of points in that interval.

<a id="stmt-shape-exclusions"></a>
<div id="shape:exclusions" class="proposition">

**Proposition 19** (Support exclusions). *There is a measurable set of full measure for $\mu$, $\nu$, and $\mu_\infty$ on which the following statements hold for every real choice of their arguments.*

<a id="shape:forbidden-triple"></a>
1.  *There do not exist $r\in\mathbb{R}$ and $t>0$ such that $$\label{shape:forbidden-triple}
     d(r-t,r)>(1-\delta)t,\qquad
     d(r,r+t)>(1-\delta)t,\qquad
     d(r-t,r+t)<\delta t.$$*

<a id="shape:slow-pair"></a>
2.  *If $s\in P_d$ and $t\in Q_d$, then $$\label{shape:slow-pair}
     d(s,t)\le c_b|t-s|.$$*

*The same statements hold almost everywhere for every positive measure appearing in the decomposition of <a href="#scale:decomposition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:decomposition">18</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shape-exclusions"><summary>Proof of stmt-shape-exclusions</summary>

*Proof.* First fix $r,t\in\mathcal D$, $t>0$. For all sufficiently large $K$, $nr$ and $nt$ are integers at every scale $n\ge n_0$ in our sums. The event <a href="#shape:forbidden-triple" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:forbidden-triple">[shape:forbidden-triple]</a> for $g_n$ is contained in the cancellation event of <a href="#can:theorem" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:theorem">4</a>, at center $nr$ and spacing $nt$. Consequently its $\mu_K$-mass is at most $$\frac{C}{\epsilon}\sum_{n_0\le n<N}^{\rm dyadic}
 e^{-\gamma tn}
 \le \frac{C_t}{\epsilon}e^{-\gamma t n_0}\longrightarrow0.$$ The last conclusion follows from $\epsilon n_0\to\infty$ in <a href="#scale:selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:selection">14</a>. The event is open in $X$. To pass to a locally finite limit, test its indicator from below by nonnegative continuous functions with compact support in $\mathcal U$. This shows that its $\mu$-mass is zero. Taking the countable union over $r,t\in\mathcal D$ and then using continuity of $d$ excludes every real triple: strict inequalities at a real center and spacing persist at nearby dyadic ones.

<a id="shape:weighted-bridge"></a>
We record explicitly the weighted consequence of the bridge estimate needed for the second exclusion. Fix a bridge $[u,v]$ with dyadic endpoints and length $r=v-u>0$. Fix a power-of-two interval $I$ in the allowed window about one endpoint and another such interval $I'$ in the allowed window about the other endpoint. The two endpoints can be interchanged. Put $$W=J(I)+V(I),\qquad H=h(I'),$$ and fix a threshold $\eta>0$. These are fixed bounded Lipschitz functions of finitely many shortfalls, and $W\ge0$. All their endpoints align with the integer times at every scale in the sum once $K$ is large. By <a href="#scale:individual-test-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:individual-test-bound">[scale:individual-test-bound]</a> and Markov’s inequality, $$\sup_{n_0\le n<N}^{\rm dyadic}\mathbb{P}(H(g_n)>\eta/2)
 \le C_{I',\eta}\epsilon.$$ Applying <a href="#bridge:theorem" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:theorem">9</a> at the integer bridge length $rn$, summing, and using <a href="#scale:local-mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:local-mass">15</a>, we obtain $$\begin{aligned}
 &\int \mathbf{1}_{\{d(u,v)>c_b r,\ H>\eta\}}W\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_K
 \nonumber\\
 &\qquad\le C\epsilon\left[
       \int W\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_K+
       \frac1\epsilon\sum_{n_0\le n<N}^{\rm dyadic}
                              M^{-\sigma rn}\right]
 \longrightarrow0.\label{shape:weighted-bridge}
\end{aligned}$$ Indeed the first bracketed term is bounded, and the second tends to zero: the exponential sum is at most $2M^{-\sigma r n_0}$ for all sufficiently large $K$, whereas $1/\epsilon=o(n_0)$. All constants and thresholds in this application are fixed before $K$ tends to infinity. The lower bounds on $M$ and on bridge length in <a href="#bridge:theorem" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bridge:theorem">9</a> therefore hold eventually; no uniformity over varying test intervals is being assumed.

Suppose now that <a href="#shape:slow-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:slow-pair">[shape:slow-pair]</a> fails for some $s\in P_d$, $t\in Q_d$. Necessarily $s\ne t$. Choose nearby distinct dyadic bridge endpoints so that their distance still exceeds $c_b$ times their temporal separation. The endpoints can be chosen close enough to $s,t$ that sufficiently small neighborhoods of these two times fit in the respective bridge windows. Some test $J(I)+V(I)$ with power-of-two length and dyadic endpoints contained in the first neighborhood is positive. To see this, choose such an interval containing $s$ in its interior. If the tests for it and all its dyadic subdivisions vanished, successive subdivision, equal half speeds, and equality in the triangle inequality would make $d$ affine on that interval, contrary to $s\in P_d$. In the second neighborhood there is similarly a power-of-two interval $I'$ with $h(I')>0$. In fact exact unit distance between the endpoints of any interval forces exact unit distance on every subinterval, by the triangle inequality and $d(x,y)\le|x-y|$; hence an interval containing $t$ in its interior cannot have zero shortfall.

Choose a positive rational $\eta<h(I')$. The resulting strict bridge and shortfall conditions define an open set. By <a href="#shape:weighted-bridge" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:weighted-bridge">[shape:weighted-bridge]</a>, its $W\mu$-mass is zero, using compactly supported continuous tests in $\mathcal U$ as above. Its part with $W>0$ therefore has zero $\mu$-mass. There are only countably many choices of the bridge, the two tests, their orientation, and the rational threshold. Every violation is covered by one of these choices, proving <a href="#shape:slow-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:slow-pair">[shape:slow-pair]</a> simultaneously for all real $s,t$.

Both properties are invariant under every translation and positive dilation of time, including the corresponding division of distances by the dilation factor. The measure inequality $(S^{-1})_*\nu\le\mu$ from <a href="#scale:decomposition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:decomposition">18</a> consequently transfers them to $\nu$. All the other component measures are dominated by $\mu$, so they inherit them as well. This also provides a measurable full-measure set: the exceptional sets were covered by countably many measurable strict tests before extension to real arguments. ◻

</details>

The local-to-global propagation below uses this zero-hyperbolic Gromov-product framework <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Bestvina1997](#ref-Bestvina1997), Section 2.2], with an explicit argument on the semimetric arrays rather than an invocation of a theorem about geodesic real trees.

<a id="stmt-shape-classification"></a>
<div id="shape:classification" class="proposition">

**Proposition 20** (Shape classification). *Every nonaffine array in $X_0$ satisfying <a href="#shape:exclusions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:exclusions">19</a> has one of the following forms:*

1.  *every secant has speed at most $c_b$;*

2.  *there is one exterior ray of exact unit speed, and the complementary closed half-line has all secant speeds at most $c_b$;*

3.  *there are two exterior rays of exact unit speed, separated by a closed interval $[a,b]$ of positive length, and every secant contained in $[a,b]$ has speed at most $c_b$.*

*The second possibility includes either orientation. These descriptions do not assert additivity of distances across the slow part. The three temporal configurations are illustrated in <a href="#shape:figure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:figure">3</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shape-classification"><summary>Proof of stmt-shape-classification</summary>

<a id="shape:tree-inequality"></a>
*Proof.* We first show that a connected component $U$ of $\mathbb{R}\setminus P_d$ is affine with a single speed throughout. Its local speeds agree on overlapping neighborhoods, and hence are a common $w\in[0,1]$ by connectedness. A compact subinterval of $U$ has a sufficiently fine partition $x_0<\cdots<x_m$ such that each consecutive triple lies in a local affine neighborhood. When $w=0$, the triangle inequality immediately gives $d(x_0,x_m)=0$. When $w>0$, propagate additivity inductively. Suppose it holds from $x_0$ through $x_j$, and use the four-point inequality of <a href="#scale:tree-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:tree-inequality">[scale:tree-inequality]</a> at base $v=x_j$ in its limiting form $$\label{shape:tree-inequality}
 B_v(x,z)\ge\min\{B_v(x,y),B_v(y,z)\},\qquad
 B_v(x,z)=d(v,x)+d(v,z)-d(x,z).$$ The inductive additivity gives $B_v(x_0,x_{j-1})=2d(x_{j-1},x_j)>0$, whereas local additivity gives $B_v(x_{j-1},x_{j+1})=0$. Apply <a href="#shape:tree-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:tree-inequality">[shape:tree-inequality]</a> to the latter pair with intermediate point $x_0$. Nonnegativity of $B_v$ forces $B_v(x_0,x_{j+1})=0$, exactly the next additivity statement. The first two steps are already locally affine. Thus $d(x_0,x_m)=w(x_m-x_0)$, and varying the compact subinterval proves the claim. In particular $P_d\ne\varnothing$ for a nonaffine array.

Suppose a component $U$ has speed $w>c_b$. It has at least one finite endpoint $s\in P_d$, since $U=\mathbb{R}$ would imply affinity. If $w<1$, every interior point $t\in U$ belongs to $Q_d$. Continuity at the endpoint gives $d(s,t)=w|t-s|$, contradicting <a href="#shape:slow-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:slow-pair">[shape:slow-pair]</a>. Therefore $w=1$. Such a component cannot have two finite endpoints: they would both belong to $P_d\subset Q_d$, and their unit mutual speed would again contradict <a href="#shape:slow-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:slow-pair">[shape:slow-pair]</a>. Hence every component of speed greater than $c_b$ is an exterior unit ray; there are at most two.

Let $I$ be the closed complement of these rays. It is the whole line, a closed half-line, or a closed bounded interval. For $x<y$ in $I$, if $[x,y]\cap P_d$ is empty, the two points lie in a component of speed at most $c_b$. Otherwise let $p$ and $q$ be the first and last points of $[x,y]\cap P_d$. The portions from $x$ to $p$ and from $q$ to $y$ have speed at most $c_b$, by the component claim and continuity. The middle portion satisfies the same bound by <a href="#shape:slow-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:slow-pair">[shape:slow-pair]</a>, because $p,q\in P_d\subset Q_d$. The triangle inequality gives $d(x,y)\le c_b(y-x)$. The only case not yet settled is a bounded slow interval of length zero; <a href="#shape:crossloss" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:crossloss">21</a> below excludes it. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-shape-crossloss"></a>
<div id="shape:crossloss" class="lemma">

<a id="shape:cross-bound"></a>
**Lemma 21** (Loss across two rays). *Suppose there are two exterior unit rays with slow interval $[a,b]$, and put $L=b-a$. Then $L>0$, and, with the fixed constant <a id="shape:cross-constant"></a>$$\label{shape:cross-constant}
 C_*=2+\frac6\delta,$$ every $z\in\mathbb{R}$ and $t>0$ satisfy $$\label{shape:cross-bound}
 0\le 1-\frac{d(z,z+t)}t\le\frac{C_*L}{t}.$$ The shortfall is zero whenever the interval is wholly in either closed unit ray.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shape-crossloss"><summary>Proof of stmt-shape-crossloss</summary>

<a id="shape:matched-ray-distance"></a>
*Proof.* For the moment permit $L=0$. Write $x_u=a-u$, $y_v=b+v$, where $u,v\ge0$, and put $D=d(a,b)\le L$. Set $$s=\frac{u+v-D-d(x_u,y_v)}2.$$ If $s\le0$, the absolute shortfall of $[x_u,y_v]$ is at most $L+D\le2L$. Suppose $s>0$. The reverse triangle inequalities imply $d(x_u,y_v)\ge u-D-v$ and $d(x_u,y_v)\ge v-D-u$, so that $s\le\min\{u,v\}$. At base $a$ the unit rays and triangle inequalities give $$\begin{aligned}
 B_a(x_u,x_s)&=2s,\\
 B_a(x_u,y_v)&\ge u+(v-D)-d(x_u,y_v)=2s,\\
 B_a(y_v,y_s)&\ge(v-D)+(s-D)-(v-s)=2s-2D.
\end{aligned}$$ Twice applying <a href="#shape:tree-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:tree-inequality">[shape:tree-inequality]</a> yields $B_a(x_s,y_s)\ge2s-2D$. As $d(a,x_s)=s$ and $d(a,y_s)\le D+s$, it follows that $$\label{shape:matched-ray-distance}
 d(x_s,y_s)\le3D\le3L.$$

Use the temporal midpoint $m=(a+b)/2$ and spacing $t_*=s+L/2$. Its two wing distances obey $$d(x_s,m),\ d(m,y_s)\ge s-L/2.$$ If $L>0$ and $s>3L/\delta$, these bounds are strictly greater than $(1-\delta)t_*$ and <a href="#shape:matched-ray-distance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:matched-ray-distance">[shape:matched-ray-distance]</a> is strictly less than $\delta t_*$. This is the forbidden triple of <a href="#shape:forbidden-triple" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:forbidden-triple">[shape:forbidden-triple]</a>. If $L=0$, every $s>0$ produces that same strict contradiction. Thus always $s\le3L/\delta$. A full crossing interval has absolute shortfall $$(u+L+v)-d(x_u,y_v)=L+D+2s\le C_*L.$$ An interval meeting only part of the slow interval has absolute shortfall at most twice the length of that part. For example, if $x<a\le y\le b$, then $d(x,y)\ge(a-x)-d(a,y)\ge(a-x)-(y-a)$, giving loss at most $2(y-a)$. The other orientation is identical. An interval inside $[a,b]$ has loss at most its length, hence at most $L$, and intervals in the unit rays have zero loss. This proves <a href="#shape:cross-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:cross-bound">[shape:cross-bound]</a> in every case. If $L=0$, these conclusions would give exact unit distance between every pair of times, an affine array. Nonaffinity therefore forces $L>0$. ◻

</details>

<figure id="shape:figure">

<figcaption>The possible temporal regions in <a href="#shape:classification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:classification">20</a>; the one-ray picture may also be reflected. A slow region need not be affine. In the bottom picture, excessive loss between the exterior rays would produce the symmetric forbidden triple $(x_{s},m,y_{s})$ used in <a href="#shape:crossloss" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:crossloss">21</a>. The diagram describes time regions, not an embedding of the semimetric.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="end:section"></a>
### A capped deficit and the final balance

The cap will compare the slow interval of a limiting shape at fine and coarse scales. It must equal one on uniformly slow secants. For a two-ray shape, <a href="#shape:cross-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:cross-bound">[shape:cross-bound]</a> makes the shortfall of a long crossing interval small; a sufficiently small slope of the cap near zero will make its averaged coarse contribution smaller than the fine-scale occupancy of the slow interval. This is an integrated comparison, not a pointwise sign assertion for the observable below.

<a id="end:cap"></a>
<a id="end:linear-bounds"></a>
Choose a fixed $\alpha>0$ so small that $10C_*\alpha<1$. Choose a smooth nondecreasing function $\Phi:[0,1]\to[0,1]$ such that $$\label{end:cap}
 \Phi(u)=\alpha u\ \text{near }0,\qquad
 \Phi(u)=1\quad(1-c_b\le u\le1).$$ For completeness, one can choose a nonnegative smooth derivative equal to $\alpha$ near zero, zero near $1-c_b$, and with integral one on $[0,1-c_b]$, by adding a nonnegative bump supported strictly between the two endpoint neighborhoods. Integrating and continuing constantly gives <a href="#end:cap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="end:cap">[end:cap]</a>. Positivity near zero and compactness give constants $0<c_\Phi\le C_\Phi<\infty$ with $$\label{end:linear-bounds}
 c_\Phi u\le\Phi(u)\le C_\Phi u\qquad(0\le u\le1).$$ Extend $\Phi$ smoothly to the real line, keeping it linear immediately to the left of zero. The extension only defines continuous tests on $X$; the arrays used below have the relevant shortfalls in $[0,1]$.

<a id="end:G-definition"></a>
<a id="end:G-bound"></a>
Write $h_t(z)=1-d(z,z+t)/t$, and abbreviate $J=J([0,2])$ and $V=V([0,2])$. Define $$\label{end:G-definition}
 G(d)=\Phi(h_2(0))-\tfrac12\Phi(h_1(0))-\tfrac12\Phi(h_1(1)).$$ If $a=h_1(0)$, $b=h_1(1)$, and $\bar a=(a+b)/2$, then $h_2(0)=\bar a+J$ and $V=(a-b)^2$. The mean value theorem followed by the second-order symmetric Taylor bound yields $$\label{end:G-bound}
 |G|\le\|\Phi'\|J+\frac18\|\Phi''\|V\le C(J+V),$$ where the derivative norms can be taken on the compact argument range of these three tests on $X$. In particular $G$ is $\mu$-integrable by <a href="#scale:limiting-test-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:limiting-test-bound">[scale:limiting-test-bound]</a>.

<a id="end:H-definition"></a>
At the terminal scale, $\int h_1(0)\,\,\mathrm{d}\nu_K=1$. After a subsequence, <a href="#end:linear-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="end:linear-bounds">[end:linear-bounds]</a> therefore gives a limit $$\label{end:H-definition}
H_\Phi=\lim_K\int\Phi(h_1(0))\,\,\mathrm{d}\nu_K,
 \qquad c_\Phi\le H_\Phi\le C_\Phi.$$

There are three steps to the contradiction. First, dyadic telescoping will give $\int G\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu=H_\Phi$, with no loss of weighted mass to $\mathcal A$. Then the positive decomposition of <a href="#scale:decomposition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:decomposition">18</a> separates this integral into a terminal contribution and the contribution of the invariant remainder. We prove that the former is strictly less than $H_\Phi$ and the latter is nonpositive. The cap’s two prescribed behaviors enter precisely in these comparisons.

<a id="stmt-end-balance"></a>
<div id="end:balance" class="proposition">

<a id="end:balance-equation"></a>
**Proposition 22** (Exact limiting balance). *With the preceding choices, $$\label{end:balance-equation}
 \int G\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu=H_\Phi>0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-end-balance"><summary>Proof of stmt-end-balance</summary>

*Proof.* Stationarity makes the two half-interval terms in <a href="#end:G-definition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="end:G-definition">[end:G-definition]</a> have equal expectation. Since $h_2(0;g_n)=h_1(0;g_{2n})$, summing over the dyadic scales gives exactly $$\int G\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_K
 =\int\Phi(h_1(0))\,\,\mathrm{d}\nu_K
  -\frac1\epsilon\mathbb{E}\Phi(h_1(0;g_{n_0})).$$ The final term tends to zero by [scale:initial-test-bound,end:linear-bounds] [scale:initial-test-bound](#scale:initial-test-bound), [end:linear-bounds](#end:linear-bounds). Thus these integrals converge to $H_\Phi$. We must show that this mass does not escape to $\mathcal A$ under vague convergence.

The measures $J\mu_K$ and $V\mu_K$ have uniformly bounded total mass on the compact space $X$, and the signed measures $G\mu_K$ have uniformly bounded total variation by <a href="#end:G-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="end:G-bound">[end:G-bound]</a>. Take simultaneous weak limits $$J\mu_K\longrightarrow\lambda_J,\qquad
 V\mu_K\longrightarrow\lambda_V,\qquad
 G\mu_K\longrightarrow\eta.$$ On $\mathcal U$ they agree with $J\mu$, $V\mu$, and $G\mu$ respectively: multiply any compactly supported continuous test there by the indicated continuous weight. We show that $\eta|_{\mathcal A}=0$ by treating all three ranges of affine speeds. Write $d_w(s,t)=w|s-t|$.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
###### Speeds $0\le w\le c_b$.

Both $\Phi'$ and $\Phi''$ vanish at every shortfall $1-w$ in this compact speed range, including its boundary $w=c_b$. For each $\rho>0$ there is an open neighborhood $U_\rho$ of $\{d_w:0\le w\le c_b\}$ on which the Taylor argument for <a href="#end:G-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="end:G-bound">[end:G-bound]</a> gives $$|G|\le\rho(J+V).$$ Indeed all arguments between the three relevant shortfalls are then uniformly close to $[1-c_b,1]$, where both derivatives vanish. For a continuous test supported in $U_\rho$, pass this domination to the weak limits. It implies $|\eta||_{U_\rho}\le\rho(\lambda_J+\lambda_V)|_{U_\rho}$. The latter positive measure is finite on $X$. Letting $\rho$ decrease to zero shows that $\eta$ has zero restriction to this compact set of affine arrays.

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
###### Speeds $c_b<w<1$.

Choose a fixed positive dyadic $R$ so large that the endpoint windows of the bridge $[0,R]$ contain $[0,2]$ and $[R,R+1]$ respectively; for example require $\kappa R>2$. Put $W=J+V$ and $H=h([R,R+1])$. For each positive rational $\eta_0$, the open set $$U_{\eta_0}=\{d:d(0,R)>c_bR,\ h([R,R+1])>\eta_0\}$$ has $W\mu_K$-mass tending to zero by <a href="#shape:weighted-bridge" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:weighted-bridge">[shape:weighted-bridge]</a>. The open-set inequality for weak convergence of positive measures gives $(\lambda_J+\lambda_V)(U_{\eta_0})=0$. Each $d_w$ in the current speed range belongs to such a set by taking $0<\eta_0<1-w$. This countable cover and <a href="#end:G-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="end:G-bound">[end:G-bound]</a> show that $\eta$ vanishes on all intermediate affine speeds. Notice that the threshold is fixed separately for each test; no threshold uniform as $w\uparrow1$ is needed.

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
###### The speed $w=1$.

Near $d_1$, all three arguments in <a href="#end:G-definition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="end:G-definition">[end:G-definition]</a> are in the linear part of $\Phi$, so $G=\alpha J$. It remains to prove $\lambda_J(\{d_1\})=0$. Fix an inverse power of two $q\in(0,1)$. For the product over $[0,2n]$, with center $n$, put $$D=n-\min\{g(0,n),g(n,2n)\},\qquad
 \ell=g(0,n)+g(n,2n)-g(0,2n)=2nJ(g_n).$$ The quantities $D/n$ and $\ell/n$ are continuous tests of $g_n$, both zero at $d_1$. Thus the condition $2q-2D/n>\ell/n$ defines an open neighborhood $U_q$ of $d_1$. Eventually $s=qn$ is an integer at every scale in the sum. On $U_q$, the truncation estimate of <a href="#scale:truncation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:truncation">12</a> gives, with its fixed additive constant $A$, $$\ell\le\ell_{\rm cut}+A,
 \qquad
 J\le qJ([1-q,1+q])+\frac{A}{2n}.$$ The shorter-interval test telescopes exactly, by stationarity: $$\int J([1-q,1+q])\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_K
 =\frac{e_{qN}-e_{qn_0}}\epsilon\le1,$$ where dyadic monotonicity gives $e_{qN}\le e_N=\epsilon$. Also $\epsilon^{-1}\sum_n n^{-1}=O((\epsilon n_0)^{-1})=o(1)$. Since $J\ge0$, $$\int_{U_q}J\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_K\le q+o(1).$$ The open-set inequality now gives $\lambda_J(U_q)\le q$, so $\lambda_J(\{d_1\})\le q$. First take the fixed weak limit and then let $q$ decrease through inverse powers of two to obtain zero. The local identity $G=\alpha J$ transfers this conclusion to $\eta$.

These cases cover $\mathcal A$. Weak convergence on compact $X$ preserves the total mass of the signed measures, so $\eta(X)=H_\Phi$. As $\eta|_{\mathcal A}=0$ and $\eta|_{\mathcal U}=G\mu$, this is <a href="#end:balance-equation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="end:balance-equation">[end:balance-equation]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
#### The terminal measure and its backward dilates

<a id="end:integrated-decomposition"></a>
The decomposition in <a href="#scale:decomposition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scale:decomposition">18</a>, together with <a href="#end:G-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="end:G-bound">[end:G-bound]</a>, gives an absolutely convergent identity $$\label{end:integrated-decomposition}
 \int G\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu
 =\int G\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_\infty
  +\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\int G(S^{-j}d)\,\,\mathrm{d}\nu(d).$$ More precisely, the sum of the integrals of $|G(S^{-j}d)|$ is at most $\int|G|\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu$, so all the subsequent rearrangements of these component integrals are justified.

<a id="stmt-end-terminal"></a>
<div id="end:terminal" class="lemma">

<a id="end:terminal-strict"></a>
**Lemma 23** (Strict bound for the terminal contribution). *The series in <a href="#end:integrated-decomposition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="end:integrated-decomposition">[end:integrated-decomposition]</a> satisfies $$\label{end:terminal-strict}
 \sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\int G(S^{-j}d)\,\,\mathrm{d}\nu(d)<H_\Phi.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-end-terminal"><summary>Proof of stmt-end-terminal</summary>

*Proof.* Nonnegative compact cutoffs exhausting $\mathcal U$, the terminal prelimit identities, and monotone convergence imply <a id="end:terminal-bounds"></a>$$\label{end:terminal-bounds}
 \int h_1(0)\,\,\mathrm{d}\nu\le1,
 \qquad \int\Phi(h_1(0))\,\,\mathrm{d}\nu\le H_\Phi.$$ For each integer $m\ge0$, subdivision and the triangle inequality give $$h_1(0)\ge2^{-m}\sum_{r=0}^{2^m-1}h_{2^{-m}}(r2^{-m}).$$ Dyadic translation invariance of $\nu$ therefore implies $\int h_{2^{-m}}(0)\,\,\mathrm{d}\nu\le1$. The same shortfalls inserted into $\Phi$ have finite integrals, bounded by $C_\Phi$.

<a id="end:terminal-fatou"></a>
Set $A_m=\int\Phi(h_{2^{-m}}(0))\,\,\mathrm{d}\nu$. Translation invariance at the shift $2^{-j}$ gives $$\int G(S^{-j}d)\,\,\mathrm{d}\nu=A_{j-1}-A_j.$$ Absolute convergence of the series implies convergence of $A_m$. Fatou’s lemma yields $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\int G(S^{-j}d)\,\,\mathrm{d}\nu
 &\le A_0-
    \int\liminf_{m\to\infty}\Phi(h_{2^{-m}}(0))\,\,\mathrm{d}\nu
 \nonumber\\
 &\le H_\Phi-
    \int\liminf_{m\to\infty}\Phi(h_{2^{-m}}(0))\,\,\mathrm{d}\nu.
 \label{end:terminal-fatou}
\end{aligned}$$ If $\nu=0$, the series is zero and <a href="#end:H-definition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="end:H-definition">[end:H-definition]</a> proves the strict conclusion. Otherwise every shape in its full-measure support has a slow interval with nonempty interior by [shape:classification,shape:crossloss] [shape:classification](#shape:classification), [shape:crossloss](#shape:crossloss). There is a dyadic $z$ in that interior. For all sufficiently large $m$, the interval $[z,z+2^{-m}]$ is slow, so $\Phi(h_{2^{-m}}(z))=1$ by <a href="#end:cap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="end:cap">[end:cap]</a>.

The measurable sets $$E_z=\{d:\Phi(h_{2^{-m}}(z))=1\text{ for every sufficiently large }m\},
 \qquad z\in\mathcal D,$$ cover this full-measure support. At least one has positive $\nu$-measure, and translation invariance gives $\nu(E_0)=\nu(E_z)>0$. Consequently the subtracted integral in <a href="#end:terminal-fatou" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="end:terminal-fatou">[end:terminal-fatou]</a> is positive. It is finite by Fatou’s lemma and the bound $A_m\le C_\Phi$. This proves <a href="#end:terminal-strict" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="end:terminal-strict">[end:terminal-strict]</a> in all cases. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
#### The translation- and dilation-invariant remainder

<a id="end:endpoint-covariance"></a>
We next give the measurable and local-finiteness details needed to integrate over shapes. A dyadic interval whose endpoint distance equals its length has exact unit speed throughout, by the triangle inequality and the Lipschitz bound. Thus the local unit-speed set is the union of the interiors of those countably many intervals. More directly, define extended-real endpoint functions on $X_0$ by <a id="end:left-endpoint"></a><a id="end:right-endpoint"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 a(d)&=\sup\{r\in\mathcal D:
       d(p,q)=q-p\text{ for all }p,q\in\mathcal D, p<q<r\},
 \label{end:left-endpoint}\\
 b(d)&=\inf\{r\in\mathcal D:
       d(p,q)=q-p\text{ for all }p,q\in\mathcal D, r<p<q\}.
 \label{end:right-endpoint}
\end{aligned}$$ Use $\sup\varnothing=-\infty$ and $\inf\varnothing=+\infty$. Each ray condition is a countable intersection of closed secant conditions, so $a,b$ are measurable. On our nonaffine shapes a finite $a$ is exactly the right endpoint of the exterior left unit ray, and a finite $b$ is exactly the left endpoint of the exterior right unit ray. They obey, for $r\in\mathcal D$, $$\label{end:endpoint-covariance}
 a(\tau_r d)=a(d)-r,\quad b(\tau_r d)=b(d)-r,
 \qquad a(Sd)=a(d)/2,\quad b(Sd)=b(d)/2.$$ Thus the three shape classes and the length $L=b-a$ on the two-ray class are measurable and transform as asserted.

<a id="stmt-end-remainder"></a>
<div id="end:remainder" class="lemma">

<a id="end:remainder-bound"></a>
**Lemma 24** (Nonpositive invariant contribution). *The invariant remainder satisfies $$\label{end:remainder-bound}
 \int G\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_\infty\le0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-end-remainder"><summary>Proof of stmt-end-remainder</summary>

*Proof.* On an entirely slow shape, each shortfall in <a href="#end:G-definition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="end:G-definition">[end:G-definition]</a> is at least $1-c_b$, so all three capped values equal one and $G=0$.

Consider next shapes with exactly one unit ray. First take the orientation with a left unit ray and finite interface $a$. For a bounded interval $[A,B]$ of possible interface locations, choose one fixed dyadic secant strictly to the left of $A$ and a second strictly to the right of $B$. Their speed ratios on every such shape are respectively $1$ and at most $c_b$. The arrays with these two prescribed closed conditions form a compact subset of $X_0$ disjoint from $\mathcal A$: an affine array would have the same speed on both secants. Local finiteness of $\mu_\infty$ on $\mathcal U$ therefore implies finite mass for interfaces in $[A,B]$.

Push the restriction of $\mu_\infty$ to this shape class forward by $a$, obtaining a measure $\lambda$ on $\mathbb{R}$ finite on bounded intervals. Its invariances, from <a href="#end:endpoint-covariance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="end:endpoint-covariance">[end:endpoint-covariance]</a>, give $$\lambda([0,1))=\lambda([0,2))
 =\lambda([0,1))+\lambda([1,2))=2\lambda([0,1)).$$ The first equality uses $S$-invariance; the last uses translation by one. Finiteness forces $\lambda([0,1))=0$, and integer translates covering $\mathbb{R}$ show that this entire shape class has zero mass. The same argument with left and right reversed treats the other one-ray class. Half-open intervals make the argument valid without any advance assumption about endpoint atoms.

It remains to consider the two-ray class. Restrict to the length band $$\mu_*:=\mu_\infty\big|_{\{\text{two rays},\ 1\le L<2\}}.$$ This measure is invariant under dyadic translations. Its left endpoint has finite intensity on bounded intervals, as follows explicitly. Partition any bounded range of possible $a$ into finitely many bins $[k/4,(k+1)/4]$. In each bin the fixed secant $[(k+1)/4,(k+2)/4]$ lies in $[a,b]$, since $L\ge1$, and hence has ratio at most $c_b$. A fixed dyadic secant to the left of the whole bounded range has ratio one. The two conditions again put each bin’s arrays inside a compact subset of $\mathcal U$. A finite union gives the claimed finite intensity. The right endpoint also has finite intensity on bounded intervals because $1\le b-a<2$ bounds $a$ whenever it bounds $b$.

Neither endpoint intensity has an atom. If the mass at some point were positive, dyadic translation invariance would give the same positive mass to infinitely many distinct points in a bounded interval, contradicting local finiteness. Define the finite number $$I_0=\mu_*\{0\le a<1\}.$$ Every integer interval of length one has this same endpoint mass.

<a id="end:band-series"></a>
For $j\in\mathbb{Z}$, let $B_j$ be the two-ray band $2^j\le L<2^{j+1}$. Since $S$ divides lengths by two and preserves $\mu_\infty$, $$\mu_\infty|_{B_j}=(S^{-j})_*\mu_*.$$ The bands are disjoint and cover the two-ray class. Integrability of $|G|$ permits reindexing $j$ to $-j$, yielding $$\label{end:band-series}
 \int_{\rm two\ rays}G\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_\infty
 =\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\int G(S^j d)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_*(d),
 \qquad
 \sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\int|G(S^j d)|\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_*<\infty.$$

<a id="end:F-difference"></a>
Put $F_j=\int\Phi(h_{2^j}(0))\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_*$. These quantities are finite. Indeed <a href="#shape:crossloss" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:crossloss">21</a> makes the integrand zero unless $[0,2^j]$ meets $[a,b]$, which requires $-2\le a\le2^j$; the endpoint intensity on this bounded interval is finite. Translation invariance at the dyadic shift $2^j$ gives $$\label{end:F-difference}
 \int G(S^j d)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_*=F_{j+1}-F_j.$$ The absolute convergence in <a href="#end:band-series" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="end:band-series">[end:band-series]</a> implies that $F_j$ has finite limits both as $j\to-\infty$ and as $j\to+\infty$.

For the fine-scale limit, use the dominating integrable function $\mathbf{1}_{\{-2\le a\le1\}}$ for $j\le0$. Off the null sets $\{a=0\}$ and $\{b=0\}$, if $a<0<b$, then $[0,2^j]$ is contained in the slow interval for all sufficiently negative $j$, and the capped shortfall equals one. If zero lies outside the slow interval, the small interval is eventually in a unit ray and its shortfall is zero. Dominated convergence therefore gives <a id="end:fine-limit"></a>$$\label{end:fine-limit}
 F_{\rm fine}:=\lim_{j\to-\infty}F_j
 =\mu_*\{a<0<b\}\ge\mu_*\{-1\le a<0\}=I_0.$$ The inequality uses $L\ge1$; possible equality endpoints have zero mass.

For the coarse-scale limit, <a href="#shape:cross-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:cross-bound">[shape:cross-bound]</a> gives $0\le h_t(0)\le2C_*/t$ throughout this band. Once $t$ is sufficiently large, all these shortfalls lie in the linear region of <a href="#end:cap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="end:cap">[end:cap]</a>, uniformly in the shape. Hence $$\int\Phi(h_t(0))\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_*
 \le \frac{2\alpha C_*}{t}\,
                  \mu_*\{-2\le a\le t\}.$$ For the large dyadic integers $t=2^j$, atomlessness and integer translation invariance make the last mass $(t+2)I_0$ (the weaker bound $(t+3)I_0$ would also suffice). Thus <a id="end:coarse-limit"></a>$$\label{end:coarse-limit}
 F_{\rm coarse}:=\lim_{j\to+\infty}F_j
 \le2\alpha C_*I_0.$$ Summing <a href="#end:F-difference" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="end:F-difference">[end:F-difference]</a> and applying [end:fine-limit,end:coarse-limit] [end:fine-limit](#end:fine-limit), [end:coarse-limit](#end:coarse-limit) now proves $$\int_{\rm two\ rays}G\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_\infty
 =F_{\rm coarse}-F_{\rm fine}
 \le-(1-2\alpha C_*)I_0\le0.$$ This argument also covers $I_0=0$; in that case integer translates of $\{0\le a<1\}$ show that $\mu_*=0$. Together with the entirely slow and one-ray cases, this establishes <a href="#end:remainder-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="end:remainder-bound">[end:remainder-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-end-contradiction"></a>
<div id="end:contradiction" class="theorem">

**Theorem 25** (Contradiction and eventual positive entropy). *There is no sequence $k\to+\infty$ along which the largest Lyapunov exponent of $f_k$ is zero almost everywhere. Consequently <a href="#main:entropy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:entropy">1</a> holds.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-end-contradiction"><summary>Proof of stmt-end-contradiction</summary>

*Proof.* Under the supposed zero-exponent sequence, all the preceding measures and identities were constructed. Combining [end:integrated-decomposition,end:terminal-strict,end:remainder-bound] [end:integrated-decomposition](#end:integrated-decomposition), [end:terminal-strict](#end:terminal-strict), [end:remainder-bound](#end:remainder-bound) gives $$\int G\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu<H_\Phi,$$ contradicting the exact balance <a href="#end:balance-equation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="end:balance-equation">[end:balance-equation]</a>.

For every parameter, the top Lyapunov exponent exists almost everywhere and is nonnegative, since the derivative cocycle has determinant one. If positivity on a set of positive area failed at arbitrarily large positive parameters, the exponent would therefore be zero almost everywhere at each member of a sequence tending to $+\infty$, which has just been excluded. There is consequently a finite $k_0$ such that, for every $k>k_0$, the top exponent is positive on a set of positive normalized area. Increasing the threshold gives the same statement for every $k\ge k_0$. The entropy formula <a href="#main:pesin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:pesin">[main:pesin]</a> gives $h_m(f_k)>0$ for every such $k$. In particular the measurable parameter interval $[k_0+1,k_0+2]$ has positive one-dimensional Lebesgue measure and satisfies the required assertion. ◻

</details>
